madam speaker, i rise as the youngest man in this parliament and the chairman of a party whose membership is over 80% under 30. i do not want to speak in general terms, as i have heard many of them so far in the debate, so i will point out a specific specific problem to the commission. many companies require that when a young person starts work, he or she has previous work experience. and i ask how can a student or a student who has just graduated have work experience? so the young man can't get a job because he has no work experience, and he has no work experience because he can't get a job. i therefore urge you, commissioner, to ensure that when companies use european funds to create jobs for young people, you oblige them not to impose any requirement for previous work experience, because that is, in my view, hidden discrimination and, in fact, if things continue as they are, the objective of what we are talking about will not be achieved.
in writing. i commend the commission for the proposals in the employment package, because they are ambitious and decisive. europe needs such bold and decisive steps today. i say this with real concern as a member of the european parliament from bulgaria, the country with the highest percentage of young people (aged 15-24) in the eu who are either unemployed or without education or vocational qualifications. at the same time, we have critically high unemployment, a contraction of small and medium-sized businesses, and mass exodus from the country with no intention of returning. unfortunately, this is not a sign of european mobility in the positive sense of the word or of strengthening the european single market, but a sign of the mismatch between the needs of the local labour market and the vocational qualifications obtained by the educational institutions. today, more than ever, we have a duty to help the younger generation, and the youth guarantee is one of the most successful and proven instruments. it guarantees minimum initial employment; quality apprenticeships and training for young unemployed people and enables them to help themselves, which is of utmost importance in the difficult situation of the labour market. we must support anything that will give confidence and jobs to our young people.
in writing. i believe that the european commission's proposal for member states to introduce a youth guarantee would go from being a good initiative to a working instrument if there was a strong commitment not only at national but also at european level. at present there are many open questions on the actual application of this guarantee in and by the member states themselves. the integration of young people out of work and increasing their competitiveness on the labour market, as well as supporting the transition from education to employment, are among the most important objectives to be achieved by member states, but this would not be possible without the use of an additional esf resource containing efficiency and effectiveness indicators. my opinion is based on the experience my country already has in implementing a similar mechanism with national and european funding, in implementation of the national employment strategy and the 'work for youth' initiative in bulgaria, providing opportunities for traineeships, further training and work, as a result of which over 50% of young people move into permanent employment. in this respect, i believe that the commission should commit itself and give clear guidance on the funding opportunities it will provide, so that there is full participation of all stakeholders. thank you, thank you!

reports. in the light of the negotiations currently taking place between the council and parliament, these reports make a very valuable contribution to the best possible management of the challenges of territorial cooperation, urban areas and energy policy. the broad support for your reports in committee is a strong signal from parliament. i am glad that the commission and parliament are on the same page on the main issues. the territorial aspect of regional policy is of central importance for the new regional policy. mr cozzolino once again underlines the central importance of cities and urban spaces, but also of surrounding regions, for the successful implementation of our common europe 2020 growth goals. whether we are talking about resource efficiency, a low-carbon economy or strengthening europe's innovation hub, cities have a prominent role to play and must be included. it is therefore essential for me - and i repeat, we will insist in the negotiations - that governments, in the spirit of multi-level governance, involve the regional and local levels, as well as non-governmental organisations, in the preparation of programming. so, as i said, planning and implementation on as broad a basis as possible. we need to make the people who are affected, involved. but cities also need the tools to do so. we therefore insist on the proposal that at least 5% of targeted and flexible manner by the proposed instruments, such as community-led local development instruments. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting is based on the principle of the free movement of goods. i can assure you that my directorate-general is working very hard to provide concrete guidance to member states and administrations as soon as possible. but i also agree with you that we need to pay even more attention to building administrative capacity in the coming period. here we have a shared responsibility existence, it has helped 23 member states and candidate countries in 49 disasters, with a total of eur 3.1 billion. europe is a community of solidarity, and i am glad and grateful that we have a similar instrument in the solidarity fund. but i also believe that we can make this instrument even better and more efficient. i thank mrs estaràs ferragut for her report, which sets out the european parliament's position on the reform of the solidarity fund in response to the commission's communication of october 2011. we will come forward with a very specific legislative proposal in the near future and ask for your support today. ladies and gentlemen, i thank you again for your work and for your excellent cooperation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. to do that, we need the best support we can get. we therefore need to further optimise coherence between policies and instruments, to involve those who are affected and to take even more account of local and regional needs. as in the past, i am counting on the european parliament, on you, as a strong partner in the future negotiations.
the committee on employment and social affairs has adopted a report on the proposal for a directive on the coordination of social security schemes in the member states. - madam president, commissioner, with the treaty of lisbon, territorial cohesion has become a fundamental objective of the european union. mr vaughan's report provides guidance for achieving the objectives of cohesion policy and those of the europe 2020 strategy, as you mentioned. the common rules regulation and the common strategic framework provide the basis for the interaction and coordination of the european funds. opportunities for community-led local development and integrated territorial investment support sustainable impacts on local communities. the implementation of projects under different funds would result in a better impact of the investments made. the bottom-up approach, ensuring that the potential of territories is exploited, should be actively implemented, together with the participation of all stakeholders in the planning of regional and local development policies and strategies. administrative capacity for local partners needs to be improved and the administrative burden associated with project application and implementation needs to be reduced.
mr president, it is extremely tragicomic how much is being proposed and done in the name of the european citizens, and at the same time how much the citizens feel more and more insecure about what is happening, how much they are more and more fed up with these new and new administrative rules that are constantly poured over their heads. the citizens don't want more legislation. the citizens don't want more regulation. the citizens who come into direct contact with the european institutions are a handful of people. the others have sufficiently well-established procedures in their national legislation and bodies which in fact mainly apply european provisions. the only thing that could come out of this new regulation is even more federalisation, even more frustration of citizens with the european project and even more rights for the administration, because the administration is best placed to use the loopholes that exist in such procedures to refuse citizens. and this is particularly true of the european bureaucracy.

in writing. − the new budget allocates significantly more resources to the energy sector than previous budgets and represents a decisive step towards greater energy security for the european union. this is also directly linked to the diversification of energy sources that supports the 'low-carbon economy'. i support the report mainly because it supports the decentralisation of energy sources within the framework of cohesion policy. secondly, because of its regional approach, as the report acknowledges that there is no single model for application in all eu regions. i am therefore against attempts to impose uniformity in the implementation of certain aspects of this policy. energy diversity requires that the many regional actors in europe - regardless of differences in administrations, capacities and experience - are able to benefit from the availability of different development programmes. the exclusive focus on individual aspects of cohesion and energy policy (such as global warming) can sometimes prevent us from making an unbiased assessment of the eu's real state of affairs. i say again that any step towards greater energy security and independence - and this report certainly is one - must always receive our support.
mr president, madam minister, commissioner, i would like to draw your attention to a more specific form of corruption in today's debate. so far, all the speakers have been talking about abuses mainly of diverting or stealing euro funds, but there is another form of abuse which is through a much more subtle manipulation of the use of euro funds, which is very much in line with the topic of the current debate, namely influence on the political decision-making process. i'd like to pay special attention to the media relationship. the european parliament has repeatedly addressed the issue of corruption in the media, influence on the media, media freedom and specifically for bulgaria and romania. but i want to offer you two examples. and, mr. barnier, i'm going to ask you to listen to me. i guess you should put your headphones on. the first example is from the newspaper "sega" by svetoslav terziev, where it says: "bulgaria distorts the legislation of the european union and finances the appeasement of the rulers. ministers pour european money into selected electronic media under the pretext that they promote european programmes. 14 million leva (or 7 million euros approximately) will be distributed without public procurement to the most influential tv and radio stations to digest the elections. (bulgaria has elections in 4 months). and in this very sensitive period of media freedom - which has already been detected by freedom house and reporters without borders that bulgaria has dropped to 80th place in this indicator - there is an initiative by your colleague nelly cruz on this issue. but despite all these problems addressed to the bulgarian government, and especially the remarks made by the european commission, such a moment of non-transparent funding is achieved. the second case is also from today, from the bgnes news agency, where it says: the european commission has asked the bulgarian authorities to do their own investigation whether the money from the eu funds was allocated to a music company pyner in accordance with the rules. and this is what spokesperson shirin wheeler said in reference to today's news in the bulgarian press. this company, by the way, is known for funding extremely raunchy and vulgar music videos and questionable music culture, which makes me ask you the question: will there be a time when we will also finance the porn industry in europe with european money, because it is part of european culture?
mr president, mr commissioner, if we do not tackle corruption, the policies we are trying to make for financial stability, economic growth and social guarantees will lose their meaning and be doomed to failure. and i'm addressing the council today: maybe it's time to drop the diplomatic tone and include anti-corruption measures in the next summits? today bulgaria and romania are under surveillance for corruption, organised crime and judicial reform by the european commission. the obviously bad results are the reason why bulgaria is suspended from schengen, for example. but are these two countries the only ones with a corruption problem? is this mechanism effective enough, given that it has deepened in our country in the last three years? two are the major issues on the agenda for all member states and require urgent solutions. first of all, there are public procurement and the misuse of european funds. in my country, in bulgaria, people no longer believe that there can be transparent rules. abuses in the field of agriculture, in the field of the rural development programme, are particular. there, orders and projects are known long before the calls for tenders are announced. the projects are won by companies close to the government, indirectly financing the ruling party. it's not going to get the money to the people it's supposed to. all this undermines trust in both national and european institutions. the second major problem is political corruption, vote buying, which is already undermining the very foundations of democracy, as happened in bulgaria in 2011. i therefore welcome the european commission's reports which we will see at the end of the year, the reform of the olaf and the european public prosecutor. but i ask you all: will this be enough, or are more severe sanctions needed?
mr president, madam minister, mr commissioner, this debate is important. as meps, we have a duty to ensure that european taxpayers' money is spent properly. we need strong european institutions, common regulatory legislation and, of course, a european prosecutor's office with the power to enforce law at european level. but there's one big catch. unfortunately, my bulgarian colleagues here have abused this important debate for electoral purposes in bulgaria, spreading false rumors about misuse of european funds in bulgaria without any evidence whatsoever. i can say that quite frankly to you. bulgaria has had one of the lowest rates of errors with european funds in the european union for several years. there's a serious control of the funds from the european union, enhanced cooperation with olaf. there was no abuse in the case that was mentioned earlier. just because i and many of us don't like a certain music, that doesn't mean it shouldn't or can't be funded with european funds. i had no intention of speaking, but it is important that this important debate on europe, on european citizens, is not used for electoral purposes in one or another member state, but that when there is really evidence, it is put on the table before the national institutions, and of course before the european institutions, and that decisions are taken in the interests of european citizens.
on behalf of the alde group. dear commissioner, dear colleagues, unfortunately, as it has become clear, there has been no significant progress on the mercosur-eu free trade agreement due to a lack of political momentum and commitment. in addition, existing regulatory measures by some countries further impede the progress of these negotiations. these are supply restrictions, the imposition of export duties, restrictions on certain agricultural products, etc. as well as the use of 'non-automatic licences', it is expected to cost eu exporters almost €45 million. negotiations are further hampered by the argentine government's decision to re-establish control over the energy sector. in this way, argentina is violating its commitment as a g20 member to fight protectionism and to keep markets open for trade and investment, and it also sends a negative signal to foreign investors in the country. despite these shortcomings, it should be stressed that there is and is expected to be a benefit. both regions are committed to respecting human rights and fundamental freedoms and share common languages. in addition, the interregional agreement is expected to create benefits of eur 4,5 billion for each of the parties. we liberals believe that human rights, democratic principles and the rule of law, as well as environmental and social standards, should be an integral part of this free trade agreement. if the european union is to achieve coherence in its external action, to reflect its economic interests and to promote its core values, clearer and more substantial political commitments must be made at the forthcoming summit between the european union and the community of latin american and caribbean states. in conclusion, honourable members, alde calls for a more transparent and consistent approach by the commission to overcome the obstacles that block the achievement of a win-win agreement between the two parties.

vice-president of the commission. − madam president, i think that everybody in this house can only agree fully with what the chair of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality has just said. it is true that one in five women in europe has suffered physical violence at least once in their life, and one out of 10 has suffered forced sexual violence. this is unacceptable because it is brutal manifestation of gender inequality – the member is right – and it is a violation of human rights that europe cannot ignore. that is the reason why we are committed to combating all forms of violence against women within the territory of the european union and in its external relations policies. the european union will thus fully support the global efforts of the united nations to achieve gender equality, in particular by eliminating all forms of violence. we regret that the united nations’ member states have been unable reach a consensual outcome at the 2012 session of the un commission on the status of women. no results were achieved, and we cannot really accept such an outcome. the elimination of violence against women is one of the priorities of the women’s charter and of the commission’s strategy which are on the table in this house. they underline our key priorities: improving knowledge about these problems, legislative action where we have the possibility to take legislative action, the exchange of good practices, the empowerment of women, awareness-raising and funding. as far as legislative action is concerned, the union has adopted effective measures in the field of criminal and civil justice. let me remind you of the directive on trafficking in human beings – which adopts a gender-specific approach to trafficking – as well as the directive against sexual abuse and sexual exploitation of children, the directive on the european protection order applicable to criminal matters and the directive on the rights of victims of crime. why do i underline these directives? because it is the first time that the european union, which now has the power not only to speak but also to act, has not only made declarations but has presented solid legal proposals: law with which we can help women in all situations of life all over the european union. let us look just at the victims of crime directive. this is a real breakthrough for victims of crime and for women who have fallen victim to violence, because it guarantees the rights of those victims wherever they are in europe. they have to be treated with respect by authorities, to receive information about their rights, their case, to receive proper support, and to participate in proceedings if they wish, for example by attending the trial. victims of gender violence will benefit from this, and authorities will have to identify vulnerable victims, such as victims of rape, and give them proper protection while the police investigate and while the courts prosecute. so it is a real breakthrough which has been obtained. we are not only saying repeatedly what we want, we are also taking action. this is important for women, and i am sure that this law will have a very positive impact on the treatment of victims, especially on women suffering from various forms of violence and sexual crime. the eu’s approach on victims’ rights has also been recognised outside the european union as an example to follow. i can tell you the last time that this happened. we have just had a meeting with the prosecutors’ offices and justice ministries of the eastern partner countries on victim protection. everybody around us thinks that this is the way to proceed: the more groups of countries around the european union that have these kinds of very strong legal possibilities for women, the better it will be. we have also proposed a european protection order to make sure that women who are victims of violence in their homes are better protected. this will complete the existing eu legislation in the field of penal law in order to ensure that a restraining order issued against a violent partner in one member state can be automatically recognised in another member state. it is about protecting women who are vulnerable. the member is so right in saying that they stay trembling inside their houses while not exercising their right to move around freely, to go to another member state. we have to stop this infringement of the normal freedom of any human being, including women who may be under stress from a violent partner. i call on the negotiators in parliament and in the council to reach an agreement as soon as possible on the european protection order as a civil law mechanism to provide women with better protection throughout europe. but passing laws alone is not sufficient to safeguard the rights of victims. in order for these laws to be effective they must be implemented, and the member states must transpose the laws as a matter of priority in order to benefit millions of europeans. the commission assists member states in implementing these laws, but it is also very vigilant in ensuring that member states transpose and apply the laws correctly. i also call on the member states to sign and ratify the council of europe convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence. we should all speak up in our member states and explain to the media why this has not been done, and ask this question of our different governments. the commission will also continue to develop targeted and consistent initiatives to support member states in achieving tangible results. further legislation is being drafted, for instance, on projects at grassroots level to combat violence against women. this is currently being done via the daphne programme, and will continue with the forthcoming rights, equality and citizenship programme currently under negotiation in the multiannual financial framework. when we speak about violence there is also hidden violence, for instance, female genital mutilation. fgm is a form of gender-based violence and the world health organisation has estimated that in the european union there are roughly half a million women and girls who have been through fgm. this may even be an underestimate as it fails to take into consideration second-generation migrants and those who are not accounted for. we should be very clear on this: fgm can under no circumstances be justified by cultural or religious traditional practices. our member states must prohibit this. i urge you all to encourage this prohibition and to encourage people to abandon this practice in the member states. last year the european parliament unanimously adopted a resolution to end fgm in the european union. this year we are moving to action. on 6 march 2013 the commission will launch a series of activities, at european and national level, to support the fight against fgm and to uphold the rights of women victims of violence. there will be a round-table discussion and a public consultation to eradicate female genital mutilation both within and outside the european union. turning now to matters outside the european union, this is also a priority under the strategic framework and action plan on human rights and democracy. the action plan supports initiatives to counteract gender-based violence, feminicide and harmful traditional practices and to prevent early and forced marriages. i very much count on the eu special representative for human rights, mr lambrinidis, who comes from this house and who i am sure will contribute to the implementation of this action plan. we continue to work on implementing the eu guidelines on violence against women and girls and the guidelines on children in armed conflict via the eu’s delegations and the member states’ embassies around the world. we are actively contributing to the global effort at un level. one of the main objectives is to address the political, religious, cultural and social obstacles that hamper women’s full enjoyment of their civil, political, social, economic and cultural rights. they are enshrined in the convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination. we are active in the implementation of the united nations security council resolutions on women, peace and security. a memorandum of understanding between the eu and un women was signed in 2012. one of the priorities is to end violence against women, we are developing with the united nations a common programme on ending violence against women for 2013 and we are looking at opportunities for a wider collaboration in 2013-2014. we also support the work of ms manjoo, the united nations special rapporteur on violence against women and the un agenda for women’s empowerment, the beijing platform for action. i could continue the list, but i can tell you i have spoken to all my colleagues who are responsible for external affairs. some of them are female commissioners, and they really take these issues seriously and really implement the ‘women factor’ and address the question of violence against women in all the activities and the bilateral talks they have when they are outside the european union. the conclusion adopted by the employment, social policy, health and consumer affairs council (epsco) in december 2012 is a good preparation for the beijing platform for action and the eu commitment on this. i hope that this time at the united nations we can avoid the north-south division which characterised the 2012 session. having said that, we will nevertheless remain firm in defending the gender equality acquis from any attempt to weaken it. we will also organise side events focusing on the regional approaches to eliminating violence against women and girls. when we are together at the united nations session, all the europeans there will make a ‘team europe’. we will go out as a real team and say ‘here is what we are doing, here is how we can help you; we will help you to help yourselves’.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, first of all i would like to congratulate you, vice-president reding, on your efforts and your commitment to the elimination and prevention of violence against women. it is high time that women stop being subjected to violence simply because they are women. violence against women is a serious violation of human rights. we must indeed strengthen our actions; the european union can and must continue to play a proactive role. the resolution will help us to express our contribution by clearly defining priorities and proposing concrete actions. you just said it. our action must continue to focus on several levels: the european level, the member states' level, the international level. at european level, we have developed, with the commission, important and concrete instruments. you mentioned them, mrs reding: the victims package, the eu guidelines for the elimination of trafficking in human beings, the daphne programme, the european strategy for the elimination of violence against women. i would like to congratulate you, mrs reding, on your initiative to organise a round table on the subject of female genital mutilation and its elimination within and outside our borders. we must do everything possible to ensure that all these instruments are effectively applied in the member states to achieve meaningful results. it is important that member states sign and ratify the convention on the elimination of all forms of violence against women. this instrument will allow for greater coordination of actions at international level and facilitate the sharing of good practice. finally, let me also remind you that the defence of human rights and our founding values in the european union is also ensured by the european union's special representative for human rights, mr lambrinidis. it is important that you continue to cooperate with the un special rapporteur on violence against women, mrs manjoo, to ensure that the policies and actions undertaken are coordinated and effective. in conclusion, i would like to say that the resolution we are about to vote on in this house is a strong political message to our citizens and to citizens all over the world. we can thus affirm our commitment to the elimination and prevention of violence against women, not only in statements but above all in deeds.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, today's debate is of great importance in addressing not only the rights of half of the eu’s citizens, but also regarding the values and principles on which the union is based. the estimated annual cost of violence against women is eur 34 billion, while domestic violence alone costs eu member states eur 16 billion each year according to council of europe data – figures that are hard to ignore in the context of a severe economic and financial crisis. this issue has been discussed for years, and despite major advances in drafting international standards and norms to address the phenomenon of violence against women, progress on the implementation side has been clearly insufficient. leadership is needed now more than ever, leadership at all levels – local, national and international – and by all sectors, including decision-makers, business leaders, civil society organisations and community leaders. we believe, madam commissioner, that you will be as committed on the issue of violence against women as you are on the quota issue. thank you very much, by the way, on that one. we know that the eu and its member states have an important role to play, and we should be at the forefront of international efforts to combat violence against women, as we were with global efforts to stop climate change and strive for a more sustainable future. is the wellbeing of women a less important cause? in order to achieve this, we also need to step up our domestic effort to eliminate violence against women. it is vital that the european commission comes up with an eu strategy on violence against women, that all member states sign and ratify the council of europe convention, and that addressing gender-based violence becomes a conditionality criterion for access to humanitarian and development aid. such efforts would certainly increase our credibility on the international scene. millions of women and girls are assaulted, beaten, raped, mutilated, murdered, and trafficked on an everyday basis. i am sure that this chamber shares my belief that, in the 21st century, we cannot remain tolerant to such a massive abuse of human rights. we should be ambitious and press the 57th session of the un commission on the status of women (csw) to adopt a set of forward-looking conclusions that will ensure the economic, social and political empowerment of women and the protection of their human rights. madam commissioner, we believe that there is a crucial and leading role for eu institutions. we will be a driving force for the conclusion of the un csw.
mr. chairman, six years ago i caused a certain uproar among my colleagues when i sent them a letter regarding the situation of roma girls in bulgaria. and to this day, that situation has not changed. markets continue to be held annually where ten-year-old girls are sold as cattle to be forced into forced marriages, where they are raped, marginalized, cut off from education and eventually forcibly converted into drug addicts or beggars or prostitutes. i strongly disagree. it's practiced only by the roma population in bulgaria. i strongly disagree that such a medieval cultural tradition can be practiced just because it is supposedly a 'cultural' tradition, in violation of all our european values. right, fellows?
madam president, as one of the youngest members of this parliament, i can tell you that when we talk about active and healthy ageing in bulgaria, there is a grotesque tinge to this conversation. a few days ago, our colleague mr farage stirred up quite a stir with a statement of his concerning bulgaria and romania. but as controversial as his statement was, it contained a truth that i will repeat again, because i have said it many times in this parliament: bulgarian pensioners, my dear colleagues, receive an average of 100 euros a month. many of them are obliged to continue working beyond retirement because the other choice is misery and starvation. for them, 'active ageing' means nothing until their economic situation has improved dramatically. and unfortunately, i don't see the european union, which bulgaria still gives more money to than it gets back, doing anything about it at the moment.
rapporteur. − madam president, the transparency of measures regulating the pricing and reimbursement of medicinal products in the union is currently governed by a directive which is almost 25 years old, and the commission has rightly identified loopholes and delays to be addressed. the pharmaceutical market has significantly evolved since 1989, and so have patients’ needs. at the same time, the financial sustainability of national health-care systems has been put under greater pressure by the combined effect of the economic crisis and the ageing of our population, of which we have just spoken. i would like to thank you, commissioner, and your hard-working staff for having presented this legislative proposal and for maintaining a constant dialogue among institutions on this dossier. it was, firstly, essential and fully relevant to include in this proposal the main conclusion of the 2007 pharmaceutical sector inquiry led by dg competition. furthermore, the delays in the pricing and reimbursement of medicines that have come to light, and which can sometimes reach more than 700 days, are not acceptable. i am convinced that fair practices in our pharmaceutical market would also be beneficial to patients and national healthcare systems. the european parliament has demonstrated its willingness to play an active role in the drawing up of this legislation with a report strongly supported by the committee on the environment, public health and food safety last december. we have also heard the concerns raised by several stakeholders, including national delegations within the council. we are presenting significant changes to the initial proposal, which included some provisions, such as the pre-notification of draft national measures or the remedies procedures, that did not appear to be proportionate to the objectives pursued. our objective has been to strike a balance between the need to set up common transparency and procedural requirements while guaranteeing legal certainty and flexibility for competent national authorities with one principle in mind: accessibility and affordability of medicines for all. we therefore call for clear timelines for the pricing and reimbursement of innovative products, with the opportunity to use health technology assessments. two other key elements for the financial sustainability of national healthcare systems are the shortened timelines for generic medicines and the non-interference of intellectual property rights with pricing and reimbursement procedures. new provisions have also been introduced by the european parliament for more transparency in the decision-making process with the requirement to publish experts’ names and declarations of interest as well as an annual list of set prices for products. i know that discussions within the council are more than difficult, and i would like to take this opportunity to reiterate our willingness to engage in a constructive way and to come up with an optimal proposal which could ultimately bring significant progress for the efficiency of national healthcare systems. of course member states are, and should remain, fully responsible for regulating the pricing and reimbursement of medicines. but this does not change the fact that we need to set transparent and fair rules on how these decisions are taken, since they will affect both the functioning of the internal market and the accessibility and availability of treatment for patients. i would like to conclude by thanking all colleagues, and in particular the shadow rapporteurs, for their fruitful cooperation. let me also thank the commission as well as the cyprus and irish presidencies for their hard work, which i hope will bear fruit in the future to the benefit of all. thank you very much for your attention. i am very keen to listen to this debate and to be able to take it into account going forward.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, many people have pointed out, with some criticism, how ambitious the commission was in presenting the proposal for the reform of the directive. yes, perhaps we have been ambitious, i have been ambitious, and the criticism offsets the other criticisms we receive when parliament says that the commission is not ambitious enough. this time, however, we went further. i think i can say, however, that i have never had the thought of violating the principle of subsidiarity, which i recognize and respect and which i consider to be fundamental to our european union. i would say that we have always been, even in the original text, within the limits of respect for this very important principle. but i cannot, at the end of this debate, fail to reiterate my total willingness to accept the compromise text drawn up under the coordination of the rapporteur, mr parvanova. as i said at the beginning of the debate, the commission regards the rapporteur's proposals as positive. when you try to be ambitious, compromise leads to being right anyway. if i had not been ambitious in my proposal, we might be discussing a text today that is less useful to our citizens, our businesses and our member states. i believe that if this text is then adopted by the council - and i am responding to the request made by mr rübig with the blue card - it will certainly also bring benefits to employment. so this is a text that goes in many directions, but above all it goes in the direction of protecting the interests of our fellow citizens. the commission's position on the amendments the commission can accept amendments nos 2, 5, 6, 9, 12, 19, 24, 34, 37, 38, 39, 43, 49, 51, 57, 58, 59 and the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection. the commission can accept amendments 3, 7, 10, 11, 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 20, 21, 22, 23, 25, 26, 28, 29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 35, 40, 41, 42, 44 (1), 45, 46, 48, 53, 54, 55, 56 in principle. the commission is also prepared to accept amendments nos 3, 7, 10, 11, 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 20, 21, 22, 23, 25, 26, 28, 29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 35, 40, 41, 42, 44 and 55 in principle. the commission cannot accept amendments nos 1, 4, 8, 27, 36, 44 and 2 and 47, 50, 52, 60, 61, 62, 63, and 64.
rapporteur. − madam president, let me first thank you for all your contributions tonight. our debate demonstrates that we are fully committed to making progress on this proposal. i hope that our vote on wednesday will send a clear and constructive message to the commission and especially to the council. just one word ahead of this vote, regarding amendment 1 from the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) which introduces a dual legal basis for the proposed directive. while recognising a public health dimension, i would recommend that we follow the opinion of the european parliament’s legal service and that of the committee on legal affairs (juri) and stick to the single legal basis proposed by the commission. i fully understand some of the concerns raised tonight and i am confident that a continued dialogue amongst the institutions is the solution towards achieving an optimal legislative framework which would respond to the needs of all stakeholders involved, whether they are patients or authorities or pharmaceutical companies. but we have to bear in mind that the timelines – an ambitious proposal, in fact – are crucially important for the accessibility of drugs. i would also like to mention that while working on the clinical trials directive i have noticed that there are shorter timelines in the clinical trials for the committees to decide whether a drug should be registered, in comparison with the timelines that the same or other authorities – with the same experts – need just to register a price or just to register a drug and put it on a list for reimbursement. i believe that there should be some fairness and parity in this. i believe that the status quo is not the answer, and just looking back at some recent developments at national level we have seen issues raised in france regarding the reimbursement of products for which the therapeutic added value and the safety profile have been seriously questioned. we still have numerous cases of patent linkage delaying the entry of generics in portugal, and last week the commission sent an objection following the pay-for-delay case and anti-competitive practices in pharmaceutical companies observed in the netherlands. these recent cases are yet more evidence that the eu proposal bringing more transparency to the pricing and reimbursement of medicines has concrete added value and we need fair, transparent and evidence-based decisions for our healthcare systems in europe. i hope that all the institutions will be willing to engage in this direction and that citizens and patients can count on the european parliament to maintain these principles in our future decisions. i would like to thank the commissioner for his very ambitious proposal. if we had a more tolerant and engaged council i would have been even more ambitious than you.
madam president, when we talk about the recovery of european industry, i would like to draw your attention to the fact that in my country, bulgaria, industry was destroyed even before the moment of crisis arrived. three successive governments have criminally privatized bulgarian industry and completely erased it from the european industrial map. the first government was right-wing, the second was centrist, the third was left-centerist. the entire political spectrum in bulgaria was systematically destroying bulgarian industry. the european union has not only been uncritical, but has encouraged this process. and today, when bulgaria is a member of the european union, the other industries that survived this cruel campaign against them, such as the sugar industry, as mrs yannakoudakis said, are now in danger of being destroyed by the rules and regulations of the european union. it is no coincidence that for the first time more than half of bulgarian citizens no longer support bulgaria's membership in the eu.
madam president, there has been a completely unacceptable and unfounded campaign against bulgarian and romanian citizens in the uk recently. the occasion is the forthcoming liberalization of the labour market. and today, students from bulgaria and romania have a harder time getting student work and internship permits in the uk than students from all other european member states and non-european countries. these are not people looking for benefits, they're looking for equality. they number around 4,600 and contribute over €30 million a year to the uk economy in the form of university fees and living costs. there is no reason for this discrimination, which is contrary to all european principles and norms, to continue. bulgarian citizens do not go to the uk to benefit from the welfare system. the facts indicate that they go to work. according to the european commission, only one out of every 600 unemployed in the old member states is bulgarian. where's the threat? when the visas were cancelled, we heard that the bulgarian citizens were going to flood the island. that never happened. according to the latest figures, there are only 35,000 bulgarians in the uk, which is 0.05% of the population, or one person for every 2,000 people. and these are people who work or study. you should be thinking about keeping them, not protecting yourself from them.
madam president, for several months now, an aggressive campaign has been waged by some member states of the european union against my country, bulgaria. the reason is the forthcoming opening of the labour markets for bulgarian and romanian workers from january next year under the european treaties. in britain, even the government is no stranger to the idea of a negative campaign aimed at stopping the free movement of citizens from both sides. unfortunately, a member of the european parliament has also joined in with a series of arrogant insinuations and false facts that discredit the country. the right of us bulgarian citizens to live and work anywhere in the european union is just as valid as british citizens in bulgaria. bulgarian students pay their tuition fees just like all other students, and therefore should have equal rights to work and work experience. and these rights cannot be the campaign slogan of any political force, nor can they patch up the tarnished image of any political leader. we demand a firm position from the european commission and guarantees that the member states will respect the treaties. otherwise, the referendums 'for' or 'against' the european union are decided in advance.
madam president, i rise to speak on the negative campaign against the bulgarian and romanian citizens initiated in the british public sphere by our colleague, mr farage, which certainly brings him short-term political dividends, but which instills negative stereotypes and hatred among european citizens, which is an unacceptable luxury that none of us as meps should afford. i find the offensive qualification unacceptable, including that the bulgarian economy is controlled by the mafia and that bulgarians will flood the uk labour market. in this connection, i would like to draw attention to two main points: first, if we want to have an integrated single market, then by violating one of its fundamental freedoms - the mobility of workers within the european union - we cannot generate growth and increase europe's competitiveness. second, let us respect countries like bulgaria and its hardworking citizens who, despite the crisis, have managed to provide a stable macroeconomic framework based on a pragmatic fiscal policy that guarantees better than the european union average in terms of debt (below 15%) and deficit (below 2% of gdp), as well as a flat tax of 10%.
commissioner, i congratulate you on the courageous reform you have proposed. i would also like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs rodust, who has succeeded in presenting a really convincing regulation. the commission has also been active in the fight against illegal fishing, and has been involved in the development of the fisheries sector. the requirement to eliminate overfishing to restore fish stocks and the ban on discarding catches at sea are undeniable achievements. in addition to regulating relations with third countries and industrial fishing, the focus on coastal fishing, which feeds hundreds of thousands of families in the union countries, is of particular importance. together with the report on aquaculture by mr guido milana and the forthcoming adoption of the new fisheries fund, this regulation creates very good conditions for the development of the sector, the creation of new jobs, the participation of women and the development of coastal areas and freshwater basins. i cannot fail to note the attention paid to the creation of regional fisheries management organisations. i express the satisfaction of the fishermen of the black sea a new region for the european union, and i am convinced that the future black sea regional directorate will have a huge role to play in the conservation of marine resources and the development of fisheries.

in writing. − i voted in favour of the text on facilitating access to finance for smes. these enterprises are the backbone of the european economy and its development. in the current crisis, smes are at the heart of the eu's economic growth and competitiveness. however, it is precisely because of the crisis that these enterprises are too vulnerable. it is important to work towards reducing red tape and making it easier for smes to access bank loans and investments. this will encourage them to come up with creative and innovative projects. support to smes also provides a better opportunity for market development. moreover, by creating new jobs, we are attacking the high youth unemployment in the union. i welcome the recommendations on the exchange of good practice between countries, partnership at european and national level, and tailor-made training for entrepreneurs so that they can know in advance how to access funding. we must do everything we can to ensure that european taxpayers' money reaches these companies and businessmen, because without strong and competitive smes, the eu will lose its economic perspective.
in writing. at the start of the third european semester, it is time to take stock of what has been achieved and to identify what remains to be done to complete the 'recovery-growth-employment' transition. in the first two semesters, we managed to create working mechanisms for budgetary coordination between member states, laying the foundations for the fiscal compact and the banking union. if the first two years were about doing things the right way, the european semester 2013 should go further: start doing the right things. employment stimulation should under no circumstances become an end in itself. labour market interventions should be driven by real needs in the economy, not the other way around. the eu-wide drive for strong financial discipline is in search of long-term positive outcomes, not populism and short-term effects. in this context, it is preferable to increase employment through structural reforms and investment in priority sectors. the low-debt policy (as bulgaria is pursuing) is a manifestation of responsibility and commitment to the young generation it will not have to pay the price for other people's mistakes in years to come. this is particularly important in view of the trend towards a deteriorating demographic structure and an ageing population across europe.

in writing. - i express my full support for the report by veronica lope fontagné. the report is critical but proposes concrete solutions to the european commission and the european council. all the proposals made lead to a smarter management of the european semester, through maximum synchronisation between the actors at european and national level. this is the way to avoid the bad examples of inconsistency, such as the failure to report on progress towards the 2020 targets in the commission's 2013 annual growth survey, or the lack of a national employment plan in the national reform plans of individual member states. the lack of synchronisation between reform ambitions, objectives and plans leads to imbalances in labour markets across europe. i'm giving you my country, where the government boasts of its fiscal stability policy without investing enough in employment. we cannot get out of the crisis if we reduce the opportunity for young people to realize themselves on the labour market, and bulgaria has the highest rate of young people (between 15 and 24 years old) without work, education or vocational qualifications in the eu - a total of 23%! in the interests of the need for synchronisation in the eu, i think it is imperative that the commission and the council take account of the european parliament's proposals.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, madam vice-president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to congratulate our three rapporteurs, mrs elisabeth morin-chartier, mrs silvia costa and mrs liotard, on their work today. the three of us have been working very hard and very effectively to produce reports which propose concrete measures for the effective implementation of equality between men and women, both within and outside the union. i would like to thank mrs morin-chartier in particular for her report on the impact of the economic crisis on women's rights and gender equality in the union. this year, in the european parliament, we have dedicated international women's day to this theme. we wanted to show the positive role of women, their contribution in times of crisis. we will never say it enough, we must work together to support and promote education, training, entrepreneurship among women. we must make sure that we give them the tools they need to build up their confidence. in addition, we have a comprehensive legislative arsenal, both at union and member state level. applying the legislation is also a step forward. three areas are priorities in our european action: combating the gender pay gap, promoting reconciliation between family and professional life, and eliminating violence against women. as such, i would like to congratulate you once again, madam vice-president, on the excellent round table on the elimination of female genital mutilation and on your commitment to the eradication of this scourge. the european union must continue its efforts inside and outside our borders, because the answers we give are for the women of the union, but also for those living outside our continent and, ultimately, for all the women of the world.
mr president, i would like to thank supporters for the incredible work they have done. i would also like to remind the house that in 1995 the beijing declaration and platform for action set targets for removing all obstacles to women’s active participation in all spheres of public and private life – and by the way, the eu reviews progress with reference to that platform. this own-initiative report considers how gender stereotypes in the eu are hindering efforts to achieve gender equality in the areas of education, media and advertising, the economy and decision-making, as well as looking at the causes of gender stereotyping and possible ways of eliminating it. the prevalence of negative gender stereotypes based on societal beliefs and attitudes restricts the opportunities for women in the social, economic and political sphere. the gender pay gap is still 16%, while under-representation of women in political and business decision-making, occupational segregation and the growing problem of women’s poverty illustrate the severity of the problem in our society and demand more effort from us. we should not be afraid of talking about highly sensitive issues – such as sexist insults, degrading images of women and pornography – and their link to violence against women and the role of women in society.
in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the energy roadmap to 2050 is an important benchmark for the union's energy policy, the long-term energy objectives and strategy on which cooperation between member states will be built in the coming decades. i thank the rapporteur for covering all energy aspects in a pragmatic and objective way, avoiding the dogmatic bias of absolutely favouring renewable energy as the only solution to our energy future. i therefore support the commission's request to extend the forecasting models to include scenarios with long-term use of coal and nuclear power plants. hundreds of billions of euros flow out every year for the supply of energy. i therefore welcome the increased priority in energy policy of the discovery and exploitation of local energy sources as the most significant factor in reducing our energy dependence. europe will inevitably rely on renewable energy as a future major energy source, but much greater coordination between countries is needed to achieve more efficient investment, more rational use of regional potential and specificities for renewable energy for the benefit of europe as a whole. the achievement of energy objectives requires a fully interconnected energy infrastructure allowing for the integration of smart grids and decentralised energy sources. i share the need for joint investment to accelerate energy integration and exchange good practices in making networks smarter.
mr president, commissioner, i welcome the fact that stress tests have been carried out on nuclear power stations using a single methodology and criteria. this is an important step towards a common understanding of the risks and safety of nuclear energy. let us not view the observations made during the evaluations as mere shortcomings. they're proof that the stress tests were serious and responsible. i support the texts in the resolution calling for the implementation of the programmes for the removal of the remarks and all reasonable proposals for achieving greater safety. i do not support those who want to artificially increase the cost and price of nuclear energy. today, the european union needs at least to keep energy prices down if we are to be competitive on the world market.
i would like to welcome from the european parliament's rostrum the bulgarian citizens who in recent weeks, through mass rallies and marches, have opposed the pernicious policies of the right-wing government in bulgaria and led to its resignation. for three and a half years, a destructive economic and financial governance model has been followed, which has led to a doubling of unemployment, a decline in real incomes, the crushing of small and medium-sized businesses in the interests of the monopolies. it was a civil revolution against the lack of prospects for the young, against the misery to which the elderly are condemned, against the authoritarian rule of the gerb party, blind and deaf to the suffering and the daily struggle for survival of the people. the bulgarian spring also got its tragic symbol. in protest, plamen goranov, a man my age, set himself on fire. his death was the strongest moral condemnation, both to the current administration and to all those who during the years of transition put personal and private interests above the interests of the citizens, who supported the plundering of the state and facilitated the draining of national resources to shady business circles. bulgaria is different now. i believe that after the snap parliamentary elections, the institutions of the state will put the people first, with the clear awareness that power comes from the people and is exercised in the name of the people.
in writing. − there is nothing accidental in the support that the european commission receives from the european parliament and the european council for the regulation of european venture capital funds (erdf) and european social entrepreneurship funds (esif). the exit from the economic crisis requires short-term and long-term measures that must be developed evenly, regardless of their different time frames. otherwise, it could lead to economic stagnation, social and political crisis. in bulgaria, we have seen this happen precisely because of the dogmatic adherence to a policy of strict fiscal discipline, but without the courage to change the unreformed public sectors. long-term measures in these areas are needed to help small and medium-sized enterprises - the backbone of european economies - with policies that favour entrepreneurship and investment. the risk of social and political crisis makes innovative social entrepreneurship even more important. the regulations take this into account and acknowledge the existence of problems arising from the lack of uniform and well-communicated governance and investment requirements in the erdf and the efsi. we call on member state governments to follow this model of crisis exit and to take advantage of the measures and opportunities already achieved at european level.
mr president, we are on the eve of a decisive step forward towards the goal we have set ourselves - fairer, more flexible, greener and geared towards small and medium-sized producers. the common agricultural policy. the european parliament's contribution is simplified mechanisms and concrete proposals. we also stated another objective - to protect the common european interest, but also to ensure that each member country feels a positive impact of the reform. that was our challenge. each wanted to protect its national interest, but each had to show understanding for the other's specificity. and all this in the context of a reduced budget, which, if not allocated properly, risks making the cap the subject of constant criticism. but above all, these criticisms should not come from those for whom the cap is - the active farmers and ranchers. there was no way to achieve equalization of subsidies between new and old member countries. but that is why i am calling on the commission and the council to look at the specifics of the countries and to support a distribution of resources which will ensure that production-related aid is not discriminatory but reflects our desire to preserve jobs. the commission's proposal is to make the cap more effective and to ensure that the farmers in bulgaria are not left behind. it is also to give the beekeeping industry a place it deserves. the flexibility between the poles is a very positive signal. with flexibility, we found a compromise. i wish the future negotiators would keep it so that farmers and farmers in every member country can finally really feel that we have worked, guided by their needs and interests.
in writing. ladies and gentlemen, the free movement of people is one of the engines of integration and development of the single market and of growth of the community's total gdp. recently, we have seen a growing number of negative campaigns in the member states, not only by extremist parties, against labour migration, with the aim of creating certain fears among the population. we have many examples of the positive impact of migration, both on labour markets and on skills shortages and on social security systems. as the rapporteur has pointed out, open and tolerant societies attract qualified professionals more easily, which is important to take into account in terms of positioning and aspiring to the eu as a potential place for investment and innovation. given that europe's working age population will decline by 14 million over the next 10 years, one of the key tools to overcome the demographic challenge is immigration. the report we are discussing today clearly states that 70% of eu citizens believe that immigrants are good for the european economy. in view of these facts, i would like to urge politicians who are tempted, especially in election campaigns, not to provoke a decrease in this support and negative perceptions, especially among european citizens.
blue-card question. – mr farage, i raised the blue card because you keep playing internal politics without basing yourself on figures. let me give you some figures. for every 2 000 british citizens, there is one bulgarian; for a town of 100 000 people, there are 50. you call that pressure? one out of every 600 unemployed people in the uk is one bulgarian. you call that pressure? bulgarian and romanian students are spending gbp 30 million pounds in taxes for their studies and living expenditure in britain annually. you call that pressure? i am afraid that your domestic politics have nothing to do with reality, and you will see that at the next elections. mr president, if we were to vote today in parliament for or against the decision of the council of european heads of state, we would reject it. the resolution we are proposing gives us a chance to start negotiations and have a different outcome in the european parliament. earlier today, president barroso said that the european union needs a multiannual financial framework. i would add, not from any multiannual financial framework. we need a framework to make the budget in line with the political ambitions of the union. very often in this room we have to go back to the basics. one of them is: can the european union help to overcome the crisis? we've heard all kinds of opinions here (some of them very strange). the answer is clear: yes, if the european union stimulates investment in economies, if it saves national resources (as happens, for example, with development aid), if it creates added value (as happens with european infrastructures, with the erasmus programme, with young people). in all these cases, the european budget creates jobs and growth. and it creates them better than if those resources were spent at the national level, and they are not at the national level (the countries don't have them). what the heads of government agreed to was a budget that was at the level of the 1989 gross domestic product. we're trying to solve the problems of 2020 with a budget from 1989 ─ 0.94% of gdp payments. that is absolutely impossible. that's why we want to negotiate. that is why we do not adopt a budget without sufficient flexibility, without a mid-term review, with a deficit that is built into it, and with all the uncertainty that there is. so i hope we get broad support for the resolution in parliament.
in writing. – convinced that a democratic, stable and prosperous neighbourhood, together with a successful enlargement, is in the very interest of the eu, we are taking more and more ambitious political commitments for cooperation and assistance. however, we can only stand by these commitments if they are supported by the necessary financial resources. i regret to see that the european council has disregarded this logic in favour of destructive austerity measures and has agreed on a more than 16% cut to mff heading 4 compared to the commission proposal. we cannot afford such a discrepancy. it would create additional risks for the reform processes undertaken by our partners which we pledged to support, undermine our credibility, have a negative effect on public opinion in these countries, and overall limit our ability to take action beyond our borders. yet another concern is the current accumulation and rollover of outstanding payment claims in the eu budget, which calls into question the ability and willingness of member states to fulfil their financial commitments. together with the council, we will now have the challenging task to redistribute the funds between equally important policies and instruments within heading 4. i look forward to the new commission’s proposals on ipa and eni.
mr president, commissioner, as you have said, we must continue to draw the lessons from what has happened in mali, from our actions and our interventions. no transition is easy, but the one mali is currently experiencing faces many challenges. on the external front, there is what mali expects from the union. internally, mali is experiencing a security, political and military process that is fraught with many pitfalls. first, there is the matter of the european union. we must continue to support the training mission and put more emphasis on military reform. the union's support is essential for the implementation of the electoral calendar. the sending of an election observation mission is necessary, but above all, monitoring and technical and expert assistance are also needed. i would like to draw attention to the regional partners and ecowas. they must play a more active and important role. at the internal level, i think there are three key words: pacification, conciliation and construction. as regards pacification, peace is priceless, but to achieve it, guarantees must be provided to ensure the integrity of the territory and the protection of the civilian population, especially women and children. as regards conciliation, i welcome the decision to set up a commission for dialogue and reconciliation. however, i would like to draw attention to those with whom we are negotiating. dialogue, yes, but not with terrorists! finally, the construction. for the state to be strong, the people must have a sense of justice. the prosecution of all the abuses committed and the role of the international criminal court must be taken into account here. the construction is our common challenge. developing our partnership, acting more effectively and more quickly, is what mali expects from us.
mr president, this is not our first debate on ukraine and it will surely not be the last. why is ukraine so important to us? because we care about the country and its people, because it is the largest country in the eastern neighbourhood, and because it plays an important role in security in the region and in smooth energy supplies to the eu, etc. we have negotiated the most ambitious association agreement ever with a third country. we are very close to signing and implementing it, putting an end to the so-called post-soviet space and turning it into an eu-to-be space. the eu-ukraine summit last month was another encouraging event in this direction, and had to be used to make further progress. however, the closer we are, the more difficult it becomes to make those small steps which will put ukraine irreversibly on the way towards gradual proximation and integration with the eu. what is the problem? mr president, this is a crucial milestone in relations between the eu and ukraine. both sides have agreed on the most advanced association agreement which the union has ever negotiated, as colleagues have pointed out. it will bring benefits for the citizens of both the eu and ukraine. the eu is committed to ukraine’s economic growth and modernisation and, of course, the purpose of ukraine’s association is comprehensive europeanisation and a move towards european standards in all areas. the agreement is a powerful tool for this transformation. ukraine is a country of great potential and it is clear that it needs to do more to meet the necessary standards and values and to address problems, including problems with selective justice. by the way, we should also show some humility and say that in a number of our countries we still face some of these problems. declarations of ukrainian aspirations towards integration with the european union are not enough, of course. however, the recent resolution of the verkhovna rada on eu integration is a positive sign and, also on a positive note, i understand that president yanukovych has today signed a special decree addressing some ukrainian commitments specified in the february summit conclusions. we have to do everything possible to more actively engage ukraine in this process and support parliament’s special envoys to ukraine, mr kwasniewski and pat cox. it is now time for us to send a clear signal of support to the country. ukraine can no longer afford to stay at the crossroads. there is no real alternative, in the long run, to what the eu can offer ukraine with an association agreement, and both sides should make this agreement possible this year. the key words are ‘active dialogue’ and ‘result-oriented engagement’. the ukrainian people deserve this. the mechanism is no longer working, and as you can see, it is being used more for domestic policy purposes than for concrete results. and this mechanism and instrument does not work because the evaluation criteria are not clearly defined by the commission; because each time the commission sets new criteria, and its evaluations contradict the previous ones; because the evaluations in the reports are outdated before they are published; because there are double standards - you evaluate bulgaria and romania at a time when france is shaken by the bettencourt scandal, in which the president of germany resigns for corruption, not to mention the current situation in spain; because you have made these two countries a target for the eurosceptics with this mechanism, and today's statement by a colleague on the other side of the table is clear evidence; because the mechanism is a convenient crutch for all the eurosceptics who do not want schengen, nor because europe is just a repetition of an anthem that is no longer treating but is developing. i believe that mechanism has been exhausted. i hope that we will soon have a new european anti-corruption legislation that we will all work towards.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the cooperation and verification mechanism between the european commission and bulgaria has evolved in recent years from a reform driver to a mechanism for cooperation rather than verification. by deciding not to monitor bulgaria for 18 months through interim reports and thus allowing concrete results to be achieved on the basis of the institutional and regulatory framework already established, the european commission took into account the political will for reform in bulgaria and the trust expressed in the bulgarian institutions. the borisov government has always taken a state-oriented approach to every commission report and in recent years many of its recommendations have been implemented - a difficult decision for the majority, which was often left alone in voting laws, such as the confiscation of illegally acquired property for the benefit of the state, the creation of a specialized court and prosecutor's office, the introduction of transparent procedures for the election of members of the supreme judicial council and the prosecutor general. the judicial system is also seen to have people who want to change it and create an environment of intolerance towards the negative manifestations in it. but the question today is, what do we do with the mechanism from here on out? because there are three main points. first, the possibility of imposing a safeguard clause no longer exists because the three-year period in which it could have been imposed has expired. for this reason, the mechanism largely loses its original purpose and becomes a not very effective instrument, both for the european commission itself and for the parties. secondly, the mechanism is increasingly being used for domestic policy debates, which is still the case today, or for the imposition of additional conditionality by certain member states on matters both of schengen and of the free movement of workers within the european union. third, as of 2013, the european commission has launched a new initiative for european anti-corruption reports. i believe, commissioner, that this would be one of the possible ways of continuing cooperation not only with bulgaria and romania but also with all the other countries. in conclusion, mr president, in view of the above and the irreversibility of the reforms carried out in bulgaria, i believe that the possibility of drawing up a strategy for the withdrawal of the mechanism should be discussed.
mr president, i rise to express my conviction that debates such as this one should serve as a record of achievements but also as a clear perspective for the future. the balance is clear: the cooperation and control mechanism has brought some positive results, but it has mostly run out. it has been a useful tool in supporting the efforts of the bulgarian and romanian authorities to carry out the necessary reforms in their judicial systems. the recommendations in bulgaria have always been taken into account. they were a good starting point for legislative texts, for implementing and developing relevant policies, for greater transparency. today, however, the situation is quite different. it is now important to have a clear position from the european commission on what follows after december 2013. we need a clear strategy to get out of the mechanism. we need a strategy that doesn't feed the feeling of second-rate countries. we all know that feeling is dangerous. two other mechanisms exist at european level, aimed at fighting corruption and reforming the judiciary. croatia is joining the european union without a mechanism. commissioner, do you not think it is contrary to the european principles of equality between member states to place some countries under surveillance by several mechanisms and others under none? our common path is a common mechanism.
