in writing. i supported this report because it is essential to provide the 11th edf with the necessary resources to meet our commitments in terms of our development aid policy. we need to redouble our efforts on the mdgs, including those related to poverty eradication, public health and gender equality. i would like to commend the work and efforts of all the acp states that have already achieved the mdgs by the 2015 deadline. we should emphasise the need to promote the exchange of good practice. secondly, we must take into account the political and security vulnerability of certain acp countries and, in partnership with them, develop policies of prevention, pacification and stabilisation which would help to reduce emergencies. third and last, we must continue to strengthen democratic ownership. we must continue to support the national parliaments and civil society of the acp states. it is the democratic control of these two actors that ensures the support of vulnerable populations, the promotion of human rights and the respect for equality between men and women.
in writing. - i voted for this report because it is a step forward towards a more flexible, fairer, greener and younger and smaller farmers' common agricultural policy. given the differences between member states, it was important to find a compromise between our national interests and those of the european union. at the same time, this report allows member states greater flexibility to take decisions taking into account their national specificities. it allows payments to be made only to those who actually work the land and supports farmers in the main sectors in my country - livestock, beekeeping, tobacco, vegetables and fruit. the report also balances the greening of the cap and the needs of farmers, keeping the measures proposed by the commission to better protect the environment, but at the same time making them more acceptable and less burdensome for farmers. i voted for this report because i think it is a good starting point for negotiations and a good basis for achieving fairness both at european level - between member states - and at national level - between the different sectors of production.
in writing. - i voted for this report because it is a step forward towards a more streamlined and more efficient common organisation of the markets. one of the main priorities of this report is to highlight the specificities of agriculture and the food supply chain. it allows the agricultural sector to deviate from the usual rules of competition. in this way we will protect our farmers, who will be able to exercise greater market power. i voted for this report because it gives producer organisations more freedom of action so as to strengthen the position of producers vis-à-vis other actors in the chain. a key moment for my country bulgaria was the preservation of the vine planting rights until 2030. the planting rights system is a tool for exercising control in the sector. the free regime would lead to the industrialisation of production and to market destabilisation. and uncontrolled planting would have devastated small-scale grape and wine producers. it was necessary to send a strong signal to the sector and a commitment to preserve the reputation of quality wines from the eu. that is why i voted for this report - because it protects european producers and european production and their rightful place on the world market.
in writing. − i voted for this report because it is a step forward towards a model of rural development in which member states develop strategies and programmes tailored to their specificities. the removal of the axes and the possibility for each country to define its own priorities and procedures is an important step towards respecting the diversity and specific needs of rural areas in the member states. the report presents numerous improvements to many of the measures. these include knowledge transfer and information activities, consultancy services, quality schemes, investments in physical assets, business and farm development, provision of basic services, village renewal and cooperation. supporting women, young farmers, small farms, mountain areas and short supply chains by extending the list of thematic sub-programmes is a key element in rural development. the improvements made by the report, among others, ensure that farmers and agricultural activities are the main beneficiaries of support. and this is an important moment to show that the efforts of those who remain in rural areas and are responsible for their development are noticed and appreciated. that is why i voted for this report - it is proof that the european union is capable of taking care of all its regions.
in writing. - i voted for this report because it aims to simplify the complex administrative system and to lighten the burden on farmers. the report is a good basis for building a common monitoring and evaluation framework to measure the effectiveness of the cap in the next period. it proposes to adjust the financing rules on the basis of experience to date, to streamline and improve cross compliance, and to strengthen the system of farm advisory boards. the main achievements of the report are the adoption of new control and sanction systems, an early warning system, the reduction of control and risk associated with the inspection procedure, less bureaucracy and a system for monitoring and evaluating the cap. the possibility of reducing the number of on-the-spot checks for member states with properly functioning control systems and a low error rate is an important point. this will reduce the administrative burden for both farmers and national administrations. i voted for this report because it proposes clearer, simpler and more transparent rules and is a prerequisite for a better functioning administration. and that is exactly what we need to achieve a more successful cap.
in writing. - mr mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i voted against the report on the stress tests of nuclear power stations, because i find some of the recommendations to be unrealistic and unworkable. i thank all those who voted against and thus avoided the creation of a pan-european monitoring mechanism for the safety of nuclear power stations, but i cannot accept parliament's approval of the transition of nuclear safety from euratom to the treaty on the functioning of the european union as reasonable. to some extent this can be seen as an attempt to denounce clauses by euratom, but a very different procedure is needed for such a change to happen. i do not see how the commission could also comply with the text adopted, which requires it to support in an open and uncommitted way the identification of the best places to bury radioactive materials, when under current european legislation this is the sole responsibility of the member states. the requirement for nuclear regulators to carry out detailed cost estimates together with operators for the necessary upgrades of power plants is also not possible to meet. nuclear regulators are not concerned with the economics of power plants, but with their safety, whatever that may cost. these are some of the shortcomings that cast a shadow over this otherwise important and essentially good report.
in writing. − more than ever, the european union needs stability, jobs and economic growth. we have high unemployment, and on the other hand, a shortage of skilled labor. to overcome this, we need better education, skills development and professional development policies by member states, the identification of new target groups and better access to higher education for eu citizens. however, it is worrying that europe's working-age population will decline from 2012 and that it will shrink by 14 million people in the next ten years if there is no immigration. i support the report because i believe that the eu should be in the global race for the smartest people in order to attract and retain talent. but this has to happen in a well-controlled and gradual way if we want to avoid the negative effects of migration. it is important that eu social security is a key element in the union's external relations and in negotiations with third countries. immigration without a strategy creates difficulties and threatens the eu's competitiveness. in the face of global competition, the eu must maintain its position in attracting the best talent. quality, not quantity this should be the position of european migration policy.

in writing. − eu-china relations today are a multi-layered dialogue covering the whole spectrum of links from economy and trade to culture and everyday contacts between citizens. this report is useful precisely because it highlights both the points where progress has been made and the aspects which are still to be developed. bulgaria, through its tax policy, can easily attract chinese investors to use the country as a strategic access to the european single market. on the other hand, the chinese market itself should provide opportunities for our business. that is why i share the concern about the significant trade imbalance and the lack of sufficient chinese direct investment mentioned in the report. internationally, despite some improvement in bilateral relations between the prc and taiwan, the south china sea islands dispute continues to raise concerns. the report does not fail to raise the issue of the need for democratization and protection of human rights, especially of minorities and different religions. the chinese leadership was called upon to reform the electoral system, improve the functioning of the judiciary and fight corruption. we can be satisfied that this was done in clear language and without compromising the values that we have always defended in this chamber, regardless of the country in question.
author. − mr president, i would urge you all to support the adoption of the emergency resolution on the plight of minority groups in iraq, in particular the iraqi turkmen. literally hundreds of men, women and children are dying each year in attacks targeted on the turkmen community, and this tragedy must be stopped. a dreadful example which represents just the tip of the iceberg of the sectarian violence against the iraqi turkmen is the horrific suicide bomb attack that took place on 23 january this year at the funeral in tuz khurmatu of a turkmen civil servant, who had himself been assassinated the previous day. this attack claimed the lives of a further 42 ethnic turkmen and wounded another 117. despite the findings of iraq’s own high commission for human rights, which acknowledges that the turkmen have been subjected to terrorism, bombings, kidnappings and assassination, neither the federal government of iraq nor the kurdish regional government have done enough to provide security and protection for all iraqi citizens in general, and the turkmen in particular, because they have been numerously targeted on a large scale. this resolution is not just for the turkmen, it is for all the minority communities of iraq who long for peace and security. therefore, the eu should work closely with the iraqi government and use all the tools at its disposal to encourage the iraqi government to provide adequate protection to the turkmen and other minorities. lastly, i would like to thank all the political groups for their support in putting such an important resolution before the plenary and urge members, once again, to adopt it, because europe has to demonstrate that it has not forgotten the turkmen community or other iraqi minorities, and that we will endeavour to ease their suffering.
in writing. mr chairman, colleagues, i do not support either the commission proposal or the report adopted in the committee on the environment on the temporary withdrawal of a large number of carbon allowances from the emissions trading scheme. the aim is to achieve an artificial reduction in the supply of allowances, thereby stabilising emissions trading at higher price levels. the ets was designed to operate on a market basis and the low price of allowances is due to the market's correct reaction to surpluses and increased supply. the surpluses came from the contraction of national economies and the decline in energy consumption. so the solution is not to withdraw quotas, but to revive economies and growth measures that lead to more demand. if the proposed measure is adopted, it will inevitably hit the competitiveness of a number of industries, because the increase in the price of electricity is inevitable. at this moment of crisis, such an increase is highly unacceptable to the industry and to the population. let me remind you of the mass protests in bulgaria in february 2013 over high electricity bills. i cannot even accept that at such a time it is possible to accept an artificial increase in the price of electricity through legislative measures. i call for the proposal to be rejected.

in writing. ladies and gentlemen, i believe that the report shows how important the added value of each euro invested from the european budget is, with a high level of protection for european taxpayers' money and tangible results for them from the invested funds. we need a common system of result and impact indicators in the new programming period to measure programme performance in terms of its effectiveness and efficiency, rather than just its financial performance. in future, the report should avoid including topics that are not directly related to the strategic management of european funds and that apply the unobjective use of the 'point and blame' policy of institutions and countries. involving national parliaments in the monitoring of european funds is a way of ensuring greater transparency and objectivity in the controls carried out at national level. this approach has become established as a good practice in bulgaria and has led to the strengthening of management and control systems, the reinforcement of the feedback mechanism with beneficiaries, and lower error rates and financial corrections in programmes (around 1%). the commission should start preparing a best practice guide to benefit beneficiaries in the 2014-2020 period, including the newly adopted croatia. thank you, thank you!
i would like to put the debate on the european budget in the context of what we have been doing in recent years and the last budgets of the parliament. i would say that the european parliament is perhaps the most transparent european institution. the aim that this parliament has set itself in recent years is to do its utmost to limit expenditure while still preserving the ability of members to work effectively and produce what society expects of them. and we see some of the results that are already a fact, related to both the travel of members and other costs associated with them. in addition, a working group is working in parliament to look for ways of further optimising expenditure in the long term. what is extremely important is that we combine our concern for the financial difficulties experienced by our citizens and institutions everywhere in europe with the effective work of this parliament. and i think that you shouldn't make party politics out of the work of an institution. all proposals that are related to improving performance, with reasonable cost cutting, will find the broadest support. on the other hand, we must be aware, as we have seen in recent years, that the european parliament's budget is often used as a platform for various manifestations of populism and political point-gathering by one or another of our colleagues. i hope this time that thing doesn't happen. the european parliament is obliged to make extra extra expenditure next year, which is linked to the end of the parliament's term of office, and this expenditure must inevitably be made. we're looking for opportunities and we're going to continue to look for ways to further constrain spending so that this spending increase comes in close to the inflation rate. at the same time, i would again like to call on colleagues to think about the efficiency of our work and that to be more efficient, sometimes additional investments are needed. blue-card answer. – mr president, i do not think that this practice that my colleague mentioned is a practice used in the committee on budgets, where i work. very often we have amendments in all the mother tongues of the members of parliament. indeed, if there is room to make savings on interpretation, it should not have an impact on the transparency and the possibility of following parliament’s work, including through the interpretation services. so i think that we absolutely need to preserve multilinguism. at the same time i think that the practice that we have now is not jeopardising that.

member of the commission. − madam president, honourable members, thank you for this opportunity to briefly present – not an assessment, but some first thoughts arising from the european citizens’ initiative. it is too early to draw any definitive conclusions, but it is very useful here today to be able to hold a discussion with you, to reflect where things stand and to sketch out a few considerations that may serve for the future. i would like to thank all the members of the european parliament who have been so active in getting this into place, following it up and promoting it. although the first 12 months of the european citizens’ initiative have not been without difficulties, i believe that we can be proud of this new instrument of participatory democracy. broadly speaking it has been a success. the positive trends of the first year suggest that we are witnessing something which has the potential to become a very popular, and possibly influential, means of enabling european citizens to get directly involved in the work of our institutions. the european citizens’ initiative gives us grounds for optimism: citizens want to have their say, they want to get involved. it is up to us to provide them with the means to do so, and – while it is impossible to predict the end-result of any single initiative – to ensure that they feel it is worth their while. fourteen initiatives are currently registered with the commission; they cover a very broad range of areas – media pluralism, public goods and services, education, animal testing, climate change, voting rights, roaming charges, speed limits and mobility. together, they have collected in the region of 2 million statements of support. but, looking beyond that, it is evident that these groups are mobilising cross-border public opinion and triggering europe-wide debates on very important issues and that, of course, is what we wish the initiative to do. it has not been trouble-free, however. there have been some well-documented teething problems, and the commission, in cooperation with the organisers and the member states, has had to work significantly harder than we could have predicted to ensure that the instrument was quick and successful. it has been a learning curve for all parties involved. let me highlight a few issues where the commission has been trying to deal with the initial hurdles, as well as clarify some misunderstandings and misconceptions. when the european citizens’ initiative entered into force a year ago, in april 2012, few expected the organisers to have such difficulties in finding affordable service providers to host and to help with the certification of their online collection systems. the commission therefore offered, as a temporary and optional solution, to host online collection systems on its servers in luxembourg, free of charge. in order to compensate for the time which was lost, the commission also undertook to take into consideration valid statements of support collected beyond the 12-month deadline laid down by the regulation and up until november of this year. as you can imagine, these measures have had an important impact on the time and resources that the commission has had to invest. it has gone to considerable lengths to provide organisers with training, guidance and advice at all stages of the procedure, and it has had to engage extensively with the member states to overcome their concerns. these concerns have now been dealt with, and we can expect future organisers to be better prepared, technically and administratively. needless to say, the commission has gone far beyond what is strictly required by the regulation to make the european citizens’ initiative a success. some of you may say that much of this work would not be necessary if the requirements of the implementing regulation were less strict. that might be true, but we are dealing with very important and sensitive personal data here. the security and technical requirements are designed to protect not only the signatories but also the organisers responsible for collecting signatures. of course, nothing is set in stone. the commission is continuously engaging with the organisers and with parliament to ensure that any decision about a possible revision of the legal framework is taken on a well-informed basis. perhaps the biggest question that will need to be resolved by the review of the legislation is whether to keep the current, decentralised system for the hosting of initiatives or move towards a centralised system of collecting signatures. with regard to the online collection software developed by the commission, critics have argued that it does not help organisers carry out their campaigns. that is entirely correct. vice-president šefčovič has said that the tools provided by the commission are designed to facilitate the collection of signatures – nothing else. it is up to the organisers themselves to market and broadcast their initiatives effectively. the commission can continue to offer technical help to optimise the preparation and use of the procedure. also, there are claims that the commission’s online collection software has not been tested; that is not accurate. proper testing has been carried out for each release, including unit tests, functional tests, stress tests and vulnerability tests. there have also been concerns to ensure that this open-source software is further improved using a community approach. steps to do that are under way – an open-source community workshop was organised by the commission late last year – and we are encouraging contributions from external stakeholders. to date, however, only two external developers have expressed an interest in participating. the commission is of course also in direct contact with citizens. the most legitimate criticism we hear is that citizens discover that they are unable to support any initiatives and are therefore denied this treaty right. this is not related to the software or the commission’s hosting platform. it is in fact the legal framework that is in default. the commission’s legislative proposal allowed all eu citizens to exercise their right, wherever they resided. however, the regulation, as adopted by the european parliament and the council, introduced varying data requirements across the member states. that means, for example, that uk or irish citizens in the eu but outside their country of origin may be unable to support a european citizens’ initiative, depending on the requirements of their country of residence. the commission agrees that this is not acceptable and has consistently encouraged the member states to lower, as far as possible, their respective data requirements. these are all important issues for us to consider, but they should not be allowed to overshadow what we have achieved over the past year and the enormous effort that we have all have made to ensure that the european citizens’ initiative is a success. i would like to conclude by thanking the member states – and of course this house, which has been so important in committing to this from the beginning and ensuring that it is a success. the commission recognises that, when you raise concerns about the functioning of the initiatives, it is because we share the same objective: reconnecting better with europe’s citizens and encouraging their participation in the political life of the union. the european citizens’ initiative is an important complement to our more traditional representative democratic structures. but, crucially, it is also an alternative for citizens who want to engage on european issues directly with the institutions.
on behalf of the ppe group. in 2013, we have two anniversaries twenty years of the establishment of european citizenship and one year of the european citizens' initiative. it's a chance to say hello, but it's also a chance to settle accounts. putting european citizenship and representative democracy at the centre of the debate in the european union just a year and a half before the next european parliament elections in 2014 is a very important positive political signal that we are sending. on the one hand, it underlines our common values and principles, but on the other, our motivation to continue to bridge the gap between citizens and the european institutions. because today's discussion is useful precisely because we can continue to work together to make the european citizens' initiative what we set out to be two years ago: accessible, transparent and successful. it has many positive aspects and you said so yourself, commissioner. it's transnational, it's the first instrument for civic participation at the transnational level. it is complementary, it can be a kind of corrective to our actions, a complement to the action of the european institutions. she's positive about it. unlike petitions to the european parliament or complaints to the ombudsman, where citizens could seek to have irregularities detected corrected, the european citizens' initiative provides an opportunity for positive input. but she's mostly active. unlike referendums, where people can say 'yes' or 'no', here citizens can set the european agenda, make concrete proposals to implement an idea in a specific way. but that's why our challenge is to make it realistic. what needs to happen from here on is in several aspects. firstly, the great challenge remains to continue to raise awareness that instruments such as the european citizens' initiative should emerge more spontaneously in people's minds as a means of protecting their interests, and this is my first question to the commission. how can we continue to strengthen information campaigns, how can we make citizens spontaneously think of the citizens' initiative as another of their own tools, to participate directly in the decision-making process in the european union? so it's necessary that the citizens believe in this initiative. that is why we must continue to actively monitor the process of its implementation. it is also necessary to strike a balance between the expectations of the people and the legislation that is likely to follow. so i think we should think about some changes here today. the rapporteurs from the committee on constitutional affairs and the committee on petitions introduced many improvements during the legislative procedure. however, we still have some stages of the process that are slow and complicated. we need to make it effective and attractive, and we'll do that by making the procedure itself easier. we understand that the european commission has been with us all along when temporary measures had to be taken to address technical gaps and problems with access to servers. we have to continue to monitor this technical aspect very carefully. i would like to draw the attention of the european commission to one more question. we have data that for some of the initiatives, there will actually be less than 12 months left to collect signatures online. what will the european commission do to ensure equal opportunities for all participants? i would also like to say that some of the initiatives need to have more concrete dimensions. only then will we have a guarantee that they can be adapted in the form of a legislative act of the european union. it's not enough to support. i would also like to focus on the role of the european parliament and the role of petitions. we can be extremely helpful in the hearings, we have experience in organizing such events. what we need to do is to continue, together with all the institutions, to emphasise the essence of the european citizens' initiative, namely that it is for citizens only.
in writing. the citizens' initiative has only been in existence for one year. in the meantime, this new european instrument is gaining popularity and has the potential to establish itself successfully as a unique opportunity for citizens to participate directly in democratic processes in the eu. so far, eu citizens have exercised their right of initiative 14 times on a wide range of issues - from education, voting rights and climate change to animal testing, media pluralism and roaming charges. the most important achievement is that these initiatives are cross-border and succeed in launching a pan-european debate on issues that are important for the future of each of us. despite the initial obstacles, i commend the commission for its efforts to resolve the problems and to find a way to facilitate procedures for citizens and to provide effective training for the organisers who collect the signatures. the implementation of this new civil society instrument can be a good example for the introduction of similar opportunities at national level in many member states, including bulgaria, where similar forms of citizen participation are not applied.
in writing. there is no doubt that renewable energy will become increasingly important because of the urgent need to reduce energy dependence on conventional energy sources and to achieve a clean energy supply for european industry. the use of over 170 incentive schemes has led to a large growth of the renewable energy sector, but at the same time a significant financial burden has been imposed on consumers and despite the subsidies, the competitiveness of renewable energy is weak compared to traditional energy production methods. the commission notes that the aid granted to the beneficiaries is limited to the minimum necessary to ensure that the aid is limited to the minimum necessary to ensure that the beneficiaries are able to meet their obligations under the scheme. i welcome the development of guidelines for reforming national res subsidy schemes on the basis of proven good practices, which will help to harmonise the ways in which res are subsidised and improve their public acceptability. however, i believe it is imperative to create a single eu-wide system of support for renewable energy based on the crucial advantages of a technology-neutral european market for renewable energy certificates and mandatory shares for renewable energy production. this is the only way to unlock the overall potential of renewable energy in the most cost-effective way possible for the different renewable energy technologies and to create competition between them.
in writing. when it comes to protecting the environment, eu countries always strive for the highest standards. european consumers and citizens must always be protected to the fullest. safety in offshore oil and gas exploration, production and production needs to be guaranteed to the highest degree because of the many risks to nature and people. with the granting of new concessions for exploration and production of gas within the bulgarian waters, our country is interested in increased control in this area. the draft directive is addressed only to member states with a sea-side access, but should also apply beyond union territorial waters. the objective is to limit the risk of accidents in oil and gas operations. the measures envisaged include sharing best practices, strengthening the capacity to respond quickly to emergencies and improving the regulatory provisions on compensation and benefits. this should ensure the independence of the european maritime safety agency, remove potential conflicts of interest and strengthen licensing requirements, with the requirement that the investor has sufficient financial resources to cover the damage of an incident.
mr president, madam minister, commissioners, we are discussing the scale of tax evasion and avoidance, with some one trillion or one thousand billion euros leaking out of the european economy every year. but i want to focus on some other aspects that are hard to find a material dimension. firstly, it is the distortion of the economic environment and of competition. so when tax evasion is done, it's usually done by large corporations that can use offshore tax havens. this is offset by increased taxes and a much greater burden on those who are budget-friendly, on those who pay their way. this is compensated by those who do not receive the social services they should receive. secondly, it breaks the transparency of the economy. so when offshore companies invest in banks, in media, in telecoms, we see that a large sector of the economy is run by shareholders whose origin, social responsibility and tax liability are also not clear. so i think that, in addition to recognizing that there is a difference between tax evasion and tax evasion, that difference should be reduced not only by criminalization but also by concrete initiatives and measures. there are those proposed. then when a company is in a tax haven, then it shouldn't be getting public contracts, then it shouldn't be getting public funds. there are such proposals and i hope that in the member states - in bulgaria there are such proposals - they will be implemented. it will also make it easier for the european investment bank, which we are asking to do this. and last sentence the european union should adopt a list of countries that create tax havens. this is essential to alleviate the burden on national administrations.

in writing. − freedom of expression and media pluralism are fundamental to democratic governance. as such, they are monitored by various international organisations which monitor the safeguarding of these freedoms, on the basis of specific indicators. the trend of more and more european countries falling into disadvantageous positions in these rankings is worrying. concerns about the independence of the media are growing. there are issues like concentration of media ownership, political interference, pressures on the work of journalists. we have discussed similar problems in individual member states here in the european parliament on several occasions. i supported the development of common european standards for media freedom. i would particularly stress the call to the european commission and the member states to ensure transparency and publicity of media ownership - who are the people who control the communication channels and what are their sources of funding. the independence of journalists is equally important - internal (editorial) and external (economic and political) pressures are equally unacceptable. investigative journalists, whose work is fraught with multiple risks, including threats of physical assault, should be protected to the maximum extent. i believe that the adoption of this document is a step in the right direction.
rapporteur. − madam president, first i would like to thank mr lópez-istúriz white very much for the excellent cooperation we have had between the committee on legal affairs (juri) and the committee on women’s rights and gender equality (femm). we had a good opportunity to present this report together to the plenary and, with enhanced cooperation, also to enhance the quality of the legislation. i would also like to express my gratitude for the explanations provided by the commission through dg justice and for the fruitful exchange of views with all the shadow rapporteurs. last but not least, i would like to thank the organisations, associations and ngos who put forward positions on the right support and protection for victims of crime. i would also like to thank everybody, including the secretariats of both committees, for their meaningful contributions. today we will be voting on an extremely important piece of legislation, the regulation for the mutual recognition of protection measures in civil matters. this piece of legislation complements the epo in criminal matters and, in order to ensure the broadest possible range of protection measures, separate legislation for civil matters was needed. together the two instruments will cover the broadest possible range of protection measures taken by member states. we need to ensure that victims are protected whenever they travel or move to another member state. our vote today has to be guided by one key principle – to make the system work efficiently for victims and to guarantee their protection by the union. our contribution to the commission’s text was guided by the objective of ensuring rapid and simple recognition, where applicable, and enforcement of protection measures. we also had a very positive discussion with the shadow rapporteurs, which helped us to tackle the problem as a whole and to take into account the needs of and specific support services for this group of victims. as a co-rapporteur for the femm committee, i would like to stress the need to pay particular attention to the gender issue. a key element for us was to make the administrative procedure more straightforward for victims and ensure that they are protected whenever they travel or move to another member state. under the new rules, any victim of gender violence, abduction or aggression who has been granted protection in one eu member state would just need to fill in a standard multilingual certificate to have his or her right to protection fully enforced by the union. we also paid attention to the importance of ensuring that a protected person who does not speak the language of the proceedings relating to the recognition of the protection measure has access to translation and interpretation. last but not least, we will stress the importance of giving the protected person better information regarding their rights and the free movement of protection measures. that is the reason why the report stressed the establishment of the information and awareness-raising campaign between the member states. we also included provisions strengthening the collection of data by member states, which would provide an evidence-based assessment of the functioning of the certificate mechanism and would allow the mechanism to be improved if needed. the continuity of specialist support services to victims of gender-based violence and specialist training for officials likely to deal with cases of violence against women is an important part of the protection.
madam president, first of all i would like to congratulate the two co-rapporteurs, mr lópez-istúriz and mrs parvanova, for their excellent work on this very important dossier. as epp coordinator for the committee on women's rights and gender equality, i would like to highlight the excellent cooperation that has existed between the juri and femm committees, which have acted under rule 51 of the rules of procedure. it is a good example of cooperation between parliamentary committees to advance our common objectives. secondly, this proposal complements the victims' package, which strengthens the rights of victims in the european union. but through these measures we are also strengthening the european area of freedom, security and justice, in which the free movement of persons is ensured. this will facilitate access to justice for all european citizens, thanks to the principle of mutual recognition of judicial and extrajudicial decisions in civil matters. this report is therefore a further step towards the realisation of our common area of justice, without internal frontiers, in which all citizens have their rights guaranteed. two key words are to be borne in mind: simplification and protection. we always talk about simplification, but this report illustrates how simplification strengthens the protection of citizens. the abolition of intermediary formalities, the rapid and simple recognition of protection measures, the reduction of the administrative burden for victims, the promotion of the use of the multilingual standardised certificate are concrete examples of measures taken to ensure that anyone who enjoys protection in one member state does not lose that right when he moves to and settles in another member state. finally, it is important to emphasize the special attention the report pays to violence against women. to better meet the needs of victims of gender-based violence, the report proposes concrete measures, such as training of officials who may have to deal with cases of violence, ensuring that victims have an accessible language, and setting up services tailored to their needs. so again, congratulations to the two co-rapporteurs because this step forward we are taking today is a step forward in the realisation of the founding principles of our european union of justice and security for all european citizens.
member of the commission. − madam president, honourable members, may i begin by stressing how sorry my colleague in the commission, vice-president reding, is to miss today’s debate and the vote later on, as she has been personally committed to this important file and has enjoyed the excellent cooperation and support of this house. secondly, parliament’s work and successive votes in favour of this ground-breaking legislation are paving the way for a tangible improvement in the situation of victims of crime everywhere in the european union. today’s vote will be a landmark, as it is going to put european union law at the service of citizens and provide them with further guarantees. it should demonstrate the capacity of the european union to help victims rebuild their lives, and the capacity of the european union to ensure that its justice policy centres on citizens and the need to provide concrete tools to strengthen their rights. we have much to celebrate but also much to strive for, and there is still much work ahead of us to make these laws work on the ground.
rapporteur. − madam president, i would like to thank all my colleagues who have expressed their opinion. we consulted many experts in non-governmental organisations and in different ministries of justice. there are, of course, differences between the member states but in this report we have tried to take all these differences into account with a view to making the victims’ lives much easier. that is why we concentrated our efforts on simplifying the procedures and the protocols as much as possible. we have tried to ensure that the civil epo provides easy access for victims and people who are having difficulties as a result of having to cope not only with their national systems but also with systems in different member states. regarding gender-based violence and children, of course children are more vulnerable if there are bad relations within the family or between the partners. we have tried to pay particular attention to the protection of children and also to take into account different protection measures in different member states to ensure they are compatible and that family members will have similar protection and can rely on european protection measures at both national and eu level. i have one final point. i would like to thank mrs reding very much for being so committed to putting forward these two – criminal and civil – epo instruments. we hope they will complement each other and work in the best interests of eu citizens. we believe she will be as committed to these two instruments as to a gender-based violence directive. we have an own-initiative report here in this house and will present it to the plenary. we hope that we will have the support of the commission. we will be able to systematically address all the issues that we stressed during the negotiations on and preparation of the directive on victims’ rights – epo civil and epo criminal – in one piece of legislation in order to deal with one of the most complicated and difficult problems of the union: the fact that 50 percent of the union’s citizens suffer gender-based violence.
in writing. − the right to free movement is one of the fundamental freedoms of european union citizens. both the institutions of the union and we as representatives of the people have a duty to maintain and develop a common area of freedom, security and justice. this is why measures should be adopted in the area of judicial cooperation in civil matters with cross-border implications. i support the mutual recognition of measures to provide protection in civil matters, as this is one of those pieces of legislation that is opening up new territory - providing protection for victims of violence everywhere in the eu. all citizens who are victims of crime should enjoy the same protection. all victims' rights and related laws must be fully applicable; at the same time, persons with a protection order must be able to move freely. this will be possible thanks to a common european database containing all orders. this is another step towards strengthening security and justice in the eu, while at the same time making european law fully available to citizens - wherever they are.
madam president, the recovery of assets by the arab spring countries is a matter of crucial importance, we have understood that. but the response to it will reflect the commitment of the transitional public authorities to their citizens, democracy and the rule of law, and also our own commitment. we know the main difficulty: this is a matter which still falls within the competence of the states and is dealt with on the basis of national legal provisions. it is, however, essential that the european union should guarantee and encourage the recovery of these assets as quickly as possible, because this is both a legal and moral imperative, but also, and above all, a necessity for the political, economic and social reconstruction of these countries. in this connection, we note three main conditions. first, in view of the diversity and complexity of the national procedures faced by the applicant states and the meagre results achieved so far, what about the creation of a european legal and technical assistance mechanism to assist these arab spring countries in recovery procedures? secondly, what about cooperation with civil society organisations in the exchange of good practice and support for these countries? continuous cooperation should be ensured. finally, we must be able to ensure and guarantee that the funds collected are used in a transparent manner and are actually used for the economic reconstruction of the countries and the promotion of democracy and, above all, that they are allocated for humanitarian and social purposes. it is on this last point that i would like to insist. international and regional cooperation must ensure that what we do will contribute to the emergence of free, democratic and tolerant societies, in accordance with the deauville partnership.

in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i support the new directive to better protect workers exposed to electromagnetic fields at their workplace. it is clear that the old directive was not in line with current knowledge of such impacts, nor did it take account of the specificities of individual sectors of economic activity. for example, maintaining the ban on medical mri would have disastrous consequences for patients. the new directive therefore rightly provides for a derogation for the application of magnetic resonance imaging. it is of particular importance that the directive be applied not only to sectors at increased risk of electromagnetic interference but also to persons who spend or work long hours in the vicinity of television or radio broadcasting stations and radar installations. i would stress that this was not addressed in the previous directive in the way that was necessary. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of radioactive waste, which is a key element of the directive. i hope that the request to study more in depth and long-term effects of electromagnetic effects, for which the commission should devote resources and monitor scientific progress, will also be accepted, with a view to responding to the issue in a timely manner, including the presentation of new proposals to eliminate harmful effects.

member of the commission. − madam president, we are here today to conclude an important piece of consumer legislation that started two years ago with the commission’s proposal on food intended for infants and young children and food for special medical purposes. in line with the principles of better regulation, that proposal had two objectives: first, to maintain specific rules for foods only when these are necessary to protect vulnerable population groups; and, second, to simplify the current legislation by removing rules that have become unnecessary, or even contradictory. let me first warmly and sincerely thank the rapporteur, mrs ries, for her excellent work on this file, as well as the shadow rapporteurs and all the members who participated in the discussions. we should not forget also the role played by the council, and in particular the polish, danish, cyprus and irish presidencies. since parliament adopted its first reading report last year, a number of trialogue negotiations have resulted in a compromise agreed with the council and supported by the commission. it now falls to parliament to endorse the agreement reached, and let me emphasise that the commission is very satisfied with that agreement. the co-legislators agreed from the very beginning that it was time to abolish the concept of dietetic food – a concept that is considered outdated today, given the evolution of the food market over the past decade. the co-legislators also agreed that the regulation should have a limited scope, covering food for infants and young children, and food for special medical purposes and for weight control, replacing the totality of the daily diet. during the negotiations, a very delicate point arose regarding rules on gluten-free foods. i am pleased to see that the final agreement provides for the current rules on these products to be transferred and to fall within the regulation on food information to consumers. i think this is the best solution for coeliacs in the european union as under the food information regulation the current rules will be maintained and this will result in better protection for consumers, since the rules will apply also to non pre-packed foods. but let me also highlight other positive elements in this compromise. these include the increased clarity that will be created by the union list of substances – this list will replace three different lists currently scattered among existing legislation – and the clear message to carry out work on lactose-free foods and to further study complex issues such as ‘growing-up milks’ and foods for sportsmen and sportswomen. before ending my address, i would like to focus on the new provisions on the use of pesticides in relation to baby foods. i share the same views as parliament on protecting infants and young children. therefore, in this context, i will now read again, verbatim, the statement made by the commission on this matter: ‘in implementing article 11(1)(b), the commission will pay particular attention to pesticides containing active substances, safeners or synergists classified in accordance with regulation (ec) no 1272/2008 as mutagen category 1a or 1b, carcinogen category 1a or 1b, toxic for reproduction category 1a or 1b, or considered to have endocrine disrupting properties that may cause adverse effects in humans, or which are very toxic, or which cause critical effects such as developmental neurotoxic or immunotoxic effects, with the objective to ultimately avoid their use’.

the fund for the most deprived responds to the objectives of the europe 2020 strategy to combat poverty and social exclusion. by 2020, we have committed to reducing the number of people at risk of poverty and social exclusion by at least 20 million. the economic and financial crisis, financial stability measures and budgetary constraints are unfortunately not helping us to achieve these objectives. some countries oppose the proposed fund, arguing that it does not tackle the causes of poverty and does not motivate citizens to self-employment and engagement. but let us not forget that one of the european union's founding principles is solidarity, and that solidarity must be felt by its citizens as they fight the magic circle of impoverishment, declining employment opportunities and increased risk of isolation. i therefore urge everyone to reconsider the benefits the fund would have in reducing disparities between member states and to support its adoption.

in writing. − i supported this report calling on the european commission to introduce amendments to the rules on road transport taxation. transport facilitation and infrastructure development in the union contribute to the rapid and safe movement of people and goods. i believe that a more detailed regulation of the current regulatory framework is beneficial for all stakeholders. the results of the commission directive adopted in 2009 defining the european electronic toll service are currently unsatisfactory. the report, which was voted by such a large majority in the plenary, calls for the introduction of mechanisms to ensure fair and proportionate charging. the basic principle of 'user/polluter pays' for european transport policy must be safeguarded - i.e. taxation should take the utmost account of the real consumption of road infrastructure. in addition to economic and environmental considerations, equal treatment of all europeans and transparency of service prices as well as maximum convenience for citizens must be ensured in order to avoid any obstacles to free movement within the union. in this context, the commission should continue its efforts to ensure the interoperability of european road charging systems and equal access to the relevant services for all users concerned.
in writing. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i voted for mr higgins' report because the existing electronic road charging system in the eu is extremely inefficient. neither the electronic toll system nor the vignette system for passenger cars can be considered as successfully functioning tools. what is needed with the increasing freedom of movement within the union is a simplified system that allows a specific electronic device located in the vehicle to be used for identification and for the payment of the relevant charges in a central office. there are no technological excuses for not doing so, so i support the rapporteur's strong call for the commission to revise its strategy by focusing on technological solutions that work together across all member states. particular attention should also be paid to the prices and sizes of the fees (toll and vignette) because it is highly unfair for a weekly toll fee to be four times higher than monthly or annual fees. separately, the difference in road user charges across countries is also unjustifiably large and unjustified. last but not least, the policy of setting restrictions and barriers in toll systems needs to be reviewed in order to ease traffic on the union's road flows.

in writing. − the four fundamental freedoms - the free movement of people, goods, services and capital - are the european union's most distinctive feature. the most tangible for citizens is undoubtedly their right to free movement within the union. it is built on mutual trust between the member states, but they also have certain obligations. through various instruments, the european union helps each country to fulfil its commitments to schengen. however, these levers do not ensure that the schengen rules are consistently respected throughout the area. this is why there is a schengen evaluation mechanism, which monitors compliance with the rules and makes recommendations. the current proposal aims to review and modernise the way the mechanism works, and should strengthen the control of members, thus establishing the principle of equality between schengen applicant countries and those already part of the area. i support the proposal because i believe that the high security standards imposed as a condition for countries like bulgaria to join schengen should remain valid for the countries that do. i also welcome the technical amendments providing for a strengthening of the powers of supranational agencies, as well as greater transparency and optimisation of the activities and resources spent. i also support the commission's right to carry out unannounced inspections.

vice-president of the commission/high representative − mr president, i am sure this is going to be a very lively and interesting debate, and i want to begin by congratulating the rapporteurs, mr brok and mr gualtieri, for a very thoughtful and balanced report. along with the verhofstadt report, you were two of the co-architects of the madrid political agreement on the eeas decision back in 2010. your commitment to and for support the establishment of the service was vital, and i am pleased to say i agree with very much of what you have written in the report. when you think back, our aim was to create a 21st-century european service to tackle the new challenges that we are increasingly faced with, to add value to the work of member states and to do more than a foreign ministry, because we have elements of development and elements of defence. our strength lies in our ability to respond to a crisis in a much more widely-ranging way, short and long-term, humanitarian development, security and political, as well as deploying military and civilian missions to achieve lasting security and prosperity. two-and-a-half years after the establishment of the service, we can say that we have a modern and operational service. it attracts the best officials and diplomats from all over europe. it is there to deliver eu foreign policy. let us be clear: nobody defined any of this ahead of lisbon coming into force. so now that we have seen the changes and they have bedded down, it has become easier to try and define exactly what we mean by this. for me there are three key elements i want to focus on. first, our neighbourhood. i said when i was asked to take office that we should be defined in the european union by what we are able to do in our own neighbourhood. i still believe we have unparalleled influence and leverage to promote and to deliver change in our neighbourhood, and we should use it. secondly, the comprehensive approach. the merger of three different jobs makes the eu a unique foreign policy partner, because we can combine the different instruments that we have: our support for the rule of law, our civilian and military operations, our development aid, our diplomacy; operations that tackle not only the symptoms but also the underlying causes of a crisis or a situation, and our capacity to remain there for the long term. and the critical mass. we are the world’s largest economy. in today’s world it matters. collectively we can achieve things that individual member states – and they all say this – cannot do. that is why, for example, it is right that the european union is leading on work in somalia, our work in serbia kosovo and on the iran nuclear file. so in approaching the review, we need to look at ways to strengthen our ability to deliver in these core areas. i am pleased to see in the report that we agree on so many issues. you have high levels of ambition, and i welcome your support for the comprehensive approach concept, the need to get better and faster in our cfsp and csdp procedures, and the importance of the role as the vice-president of the commission to ensure the overall coherence of the external aspects of our policies. similarly, i am encouraged by the positive input to the review from member states which goes mainly in the same direction, and our eeas success rests on our capacity to bring together the institutions and contributions to maximise influence and at the same time to reduce costs to our citizens and member states. you will see much in the review that i will present in the next few weeks that is in common with this report. it is my intention not only to highlight what works, but to think about what we could improve in the short term or for the future. i want to look for quick wins, but i also want to point to medium-term changes that will require some adjustment ready for the next change of mandate. these recommendations will be designed to improve further the effectiveness of our service. it is widely judged to have delivered a step change in our foreign policy. but we should not delude ourselves – lisbon left cfsp as intergovernmental and subject to unanimity decision-making. in situations, i have to say, where there is an absence of political will or an agreement amongst member states, there are limits to what the service can deliver. one of the key tasks is not only to act as our institutional memory on foreign policy and the home of our global network but to provide, when we can, a clear, authoritative lead in building consensus between member states on strategies and priorities, working closely with you and with the commission. in that, i respect the service which supports me in my role, both as high representative and as vice-president, so that we can do this coordination work which the report highlights as extremely important. partnership with parliament is a very important part of the success, and the agreement we have on political accountability has brought considerable improvements. we have a very successful practice of exchange of views with newly-appointed heads of delegations and eusrs – valued, i believe, by parliament and by me and by those individuals who appear before you. excellent cooperation on election observation missions: i pay tribute to parliament for the work you do on this and the intensified dialogue at all levels, including in sensitive areas requiring the sharing of classified information. i have encouraged the senior management team to be open to you in every way possible. i am glad they regularly participate in your meetings and events – more than 100 events in 2012 alone. and i pay tribute to the heads of delegations who do such great work in supporting the official visits for members of this house. but i am very open to looking at ways to continue to improve that cooperation. in the report itself, i want to just focus on three particular areas. the first is cooperation with the commission, then deputies, and the structure and operation of the service. a number of questions have been raised about cooperation between the eeas and the commission and have been referred to in the opening statements. my own view is that sometimes we underestimate what good progress we have made in finding good solutions to the changes that were needed following the decision to set up the eeas as a self-standing body. looking back, i wonder if we did not underestimate just how much work that would take. but, in line with the recommendations of the report, this is an area that we are continuing to discuss with the commission, including the question of making more frequent and effective use of the group of external relations commissioners under my chairmanship. i should add though that there is already extensive work that i do together with commissioners. commissioner cioloş on agriculture, where we are working together on mongolia and china. he will play a vital role in our myanmar/burma task force in november. cecilia malmström, neelie kroes and i jointly put together the cyber-security strategy. antonio tajani is a good close collaborator on our taskforces, bringing hundreds of business leaders in support of the economies of countries in transition. michel barnier and i, who are engaged in support of the european council discussion on the defence industry. kristalina georgieva, heavily engaged in our response to the crisis in syria and elsewhere. and the day-to-day collaboration with andris piebalgs and stefan füle. i pay tribute to all of them. but i am glad your report emphasises the role of the eeas in defining the strategic orientation of external financial instruments so we get coherence between foreign policy and development policy objectives. on the second issue, i also agree also there is a need for greater clarity on the question of who deputises for the high representative/vice-president, including in parliament. in line with your report, i think we should include in a more structured way empowering commissioners to do that representation fully in parliamentary matters and internationally. and we need to go further in what already happens, mandating specific missions to foreign ministers of the member states, so that we boost the collective impact of the union’s positions. honourable members will have seen groups of foreign ministers travelling together to deliver messages as a team on behalf of the european union and on behalf of their own countries too. finally, to turn to the structure and operation of the service. we have come a long way – i am glad it has been recognised. but it is still very much work in progress. i welcome the emphasis on getting the most from our civilian and military missions. we deploy more than 7 000 people in these missions in pristina, mogadishu, tripoli, bamako, kabul, and baghdad. i strongly support your proposals to improve the speed and effectiveness of operational decision-making in csfp and csdp, and the integration of the responsible services in the structure. we have already been discussing simplifying procedures with member states. this is going to be a very important area as we prepare for the european council in december. i also agree we need to look at the internal structure and to clarify reporting lines, particularly given the budgetary situation. already in the 2014 budget i will propose a reduction in the number of senior posts at ad 16 and ad15, and i believe there should be scope to go further in this direction in the context of the institutional transition at the end of next year. staffing issues of course draw attention, both in your report and in the contributions from member states. the quality of our people is what will determine our success, and i have been very lucky in the quality of the people, not least in the man sitting next to me here and in the people behind me. i would like to thank pierre vimont and the management team for all of the hard work that they have undertaken over the course of the last three years. the annual staffing report that we will soon present to parliament will confirm the good progress that we have made on ensuring a meaningful presence of all member states and a clear improvement in the presence of women in key management positions in delegations. 18% of eeas staff are now from member states who joined since 2004, which is very close to the 20% share in the overall population of the eu. this figure also reflects this very small presence of officials from these countries who transferred from the commission and the council. gender balance was and is and will remain a top priority for me. when i took office there were only ten women leading delegations; today there are 24 – an increase of well over 100% – and i have announced a further four appointments of women already in the 2013 package of heads of delegation. helga schmid, our political director and deputy secretary general, works very actively in promoting networks of support for women in the service. we are now also very close to the one-third target for the recruitment of national diplomats. against that background, your report rightly highlights the importance of ensuring a strong presence of permanent officials. i agree. we need a human resources strategy that offers attractive and rewarding career prospects for all eeas staff, including the officials who joined the service from the commission and the council. i am very pleased that next month we will be opening publications of eeas posts also to officials from parliament and other institutions, in line with the provisions of the eeas decision, as well of course to diplomats from croatia. of course, our level of ambition is inevitably constrained by resources, and the review will need to take account of the budgetary situation. i am grateful for the support parliament has given us. you know that the bulk of staff and other resources were inherited from the commission, from the council, and we still largely rely on the commission for its corporate support. we have eliminated duplication. we have streamlined in brussels to provide resources for new tasks and, as i have said, the eeas is increasingly expected to be present and to remain in places that are high-risk as well as to respond to changes that are going on – new delegations in libya and myanmar. but in many cases this comes with heavy and unavoidable security costs. eur 30 million per year for kabul, libya, baghdad, and other high-risk places. i am convinced we need to look and transform the terms of the budget debate with member states to look at the potential for savings for national diplomatic services as we make progress in areas like shared reporting, co-location in ‘europe houses’ and similar arrangements, working together more generally in the field and on the ground. resources are also the key to any discussion on new areas of activity, including consular protection activities or more security experts in delegations – areas where at the moment we have limited expertise or competence. these changes will not happen overnight. in conclusion, can i welcome the report? you will see much in common, as i have said, with my review proposals. we have gone from not having a service to a service that is functioning. i am truly grateful for the supportive role of the european parliament and members of this european parliament in the creation of this service. i hope you will continue to make what is a very necessary and positive contribution to this collective challenge through to the end of my mandate and well beyond.
the rapporteur on the opinion of the committee on budgets. - for the last two years we have been structuring the external action service. today it is time to look at its future development as a pillar of pan-european diplomacy. i therefore believe that the allocation and funding of the european delegations should depend on the strategic interests of the union. only clear priorities will allow for an optimal and efficient allocation of resources and experts, thus protecting european interests and making europe a key player in world politics. given the need for budget cuts, i believe it is essential that the external action service and the member states' foreign ministries cooperate more closely both in operational actions and through the exchange of experience. only in this way will the coherence of european action and the continuity of european priorities be ensured. in conclusion, i would like to urge the office's management to continue its efforts to achieve a geographical balance in the institution by hiring more staff from central and eastern europe, and to congratulate ms ashton on her strong support for gender equality. ladies and gentlemen, this debate is not just an inter-institutional exercise in rhetoric. it is an opportunity to reflect on the future of the european union on the international stage.
mr president, the annual report on human rights is always of the utmost importance. on the one hand, it gives us an annual assessment of the situation in the world, but on the other hand, it shows each time the deep, lasting and serious commitment of our union to defending universal values. the syrian case, the congolese case and other cases of concern to us highlight at least two priorities. firstly, we need, together with the international community, to have functional early warning mechanisms and operational instruments to prevent human rights violations. we have a special representative of the union for human rights. it is necessary to continue to provide our special representative with financial, human and logistical resources to increase the visibility of his work and thus meet the expectations of society, that is, consistency and political strength in our work for human rights. secondly, we must strengthen the fight against impunity. on this point, what is the european union's position on states which refuse to cooperate with the international criminal court? we must encourage those who are working to strengthen the sense of justice among the victims of human rights violations. but we should also not hesitate to send clear messages when this is not the case.
the creation of guidelines to promote freedom of religion or belief is an important initiative of the european union. their fields of application are intersected by different aspects and interests. the most important thing is to ensure freedom of religious belief. the implementation of the guidelines should also take into account national human rights mechanisms which, while maintaining the map of religious diversity, should allow for tolerance of religious beliefs. sometimes it is the diversity, especially the different newly formed religious movements, that can create social discomfort for certain groups of the population. citizens must be sufficiently informed and educated to avoid any speculation, deliberate violation of their rights by religious structures. parents can pass on their religious beliefs to their children. they are responsible to some extent for religious education, but not a few in this regard can make various organizations and structures, not all of which are well-intentioned. there are a number of examples of religious beliefs being passed on in the family that are not favourable to children, as well as examples of different religious structures having a negative influence and violating citizens' rights.
