member of the commission. − madam president, honourable members, it is rather a pity that it is definitely the epa question which splits this house. i will just try, maybe not to bridge this divide, but to take the emotion out of it. indeed the trade rules are decided by the wto. the wto system may be good or bad but it is acceptable to all partners, and they are part of the wto. the best thing for development would definitely be to agree on the doha development round; it is quite clear that this would be the best way forward. unfortunately the negotiations have stalled, and then we have a situation where the agreement on epas which was agreed with our partners cannot be tolerated any more because it is not wto-compatible. but we need to move forward to make the situation clear and legally sound, because we always say that we are under the rule of law, so we need a situation of legal certainty. on top of this, if we look for investments, investments come when the legal situation is clear, also for trade matters. so having said this, i would say that in my opinion it is a pragmatic way forward. it would definitely be better if it were regional. there is no question about it; it is quite clear that it would be stronger if endorsed by our partners, and definitely it would be good if they could see that trade with the union is a way to bring them out of poverty. because nothing stands still. china’s trade with cameroon has definitely increased enormously; it is very clear that there are trading partners which exist and will continue to exist in this world. so it is important also to see how our partners see epas. if you listen to what they say in public, they will be always negative. when you speak in private or in one-to-one negotiations – like in the recent eu-acp ministerial meeting, or commission-to-commission meetings or meetings with heads of state and government – the attitude is not so clearly negative. no one would enforce epas if our partners did not also support the process, but basically they do; it needs more time, more time really to evolve. as i said, the systems that we have today are not legally compatible with the wto system, so we need to move forward. i expect that we could conclude a regional epa in central africa in a couple of months because there is some movement of our partners towards a commission-to-commission meeting with the new trade commissioner for the african union. she spelt out very concretely areas where they have worries, and for the first time i heard where exactly the worries are. in my contacts with partner countries, i have always said: ‘say what you do not like; what you need for us to change, because i would not like any agreement that hampers your development’. but i honestly believe that this agreement definitely does not hamper cameroon’s development – actually it helps them. i think that it is a pity that epas are demonised to such an extent, and that people immediately on hearing ‘epa’ believe that we are trying to impose something on our partner countries. it is not true: we are really trying to support their development as much as we can in the international trade system which exists today.
the rapporteur. − i am pleased that we have reached the point, after more than a year of work, where the house will be considering the directive and hopefully voting on the directive on the re-use of public information. it wasn't an easy journey. there were a great many issues that we discussed, negotiated and resolved with the member states, and i am pleased that the directive that we are putting to the vote today will indeed allow for a significant and visible development in the field of public information and public administration in general. the public sector is the largest source of information. so everything that is decided in relation to this information and the public sector has an impact on the transparency of the administration, on the one hand, it allows citizens to see what the motives are when one or another decision is taken, but on the other hand this information has a certain, and a strong market influence. this information makes it possible to oppose monopolies, abuse of market position, gives citizens much more democratic access to information and decision-making opportunities. what does this directive change? firstly, the information provided to citizens is much larger than before. the goal when we worked on this directive was to change the attitude of public administrations -- not to decide what to put on the internet and what to make available, but to put everything they have on the internet and to only retain information that may be related to personal data or classified information or copyright or any other restrictions they may have. but in general, information should be put on the internet and available to everyone. it was not an easy decision on the part of the states. administrations normally have a resistance to full transparency. the scope of information, internal documents, etc. will be decided by the member countries. but what's important is the rule that there should be clear explanations for why some information is not made public and available. secondly, this information must be free or at a very low cost. the society has already paid for the collection, it's not new. anyway, the public administration has it assembled. so the revenue that's coming in from different administrations related to this information should be offset by other sources. and a study by the european commission shows that if you take the overall balance of the business opportunities of such information in different areas, different phone applications - whether it's urban transport, weather forecasting, financial services, etc. - and the direct revenue losses in the administration, the balance is obviously in favour of this market opening up and creating new jobs. eur 140 billion is the potential impact that the commission estimates per year if this directive enters into force. secondly, in each country, an institution is being set up that can make decisions very quickly and, if information is denied somewhere, help citizens get it. it is very important that in this directive we introduce for the first time the principle of the open format. the data format that is used should allow different technologies, different search engines to use it, so that there is no monopoly, no preference for certain technologies. and this definition is very similar to the definition that we have in the united states, which opens up even more the market that we're creating in europe. finally, this directive also covers some cultural institutions for the first time, such as archives, museums and libraries. but i want to say that it does not in any way infringe their intellectual property rights. it does not override any of the existing rules, so moving in this direction will only allow for greater access to cultural information. i thank everyone we have worked with, the irish presidency and colleagues salvador sedo and amelia andersdotter of the epp group and the greens, who worked with us on this report.
member of the commission. − madam president, i will be very short. i will just repeat what i have said already at the opening. the commission welcomes the compromise found on the revised directive on the re-use of public sector information and looks forward to its adoption by parliament. again, many thanks to the rapporteur, mr kalfin, and also to all the shadow rapporteurs, for their tremendous work. i believe the compromise is really a good basis on which to go forward.
rapporteur. − madam president, i would like to thank all colleagues for taking part in this debate. i am also very happy to hear that the general mood in the house is to support the vote on this directive. it marks a huge step forward. of course when we speak about new technologies, and about the internet, we have different opinions. i can understand the colleagues who would like to see something much further-reaching, but i am absolutely convinced that, if we do not have an agreement now, then we will not have a directive any time soon. if we want to change public information, the digital market, the digital economy in europe and the tools of democracy, then we have to adopt this now. it is a huge step forwards. we also have to understand the member states. very often it will mean changing administrative procedures and administrative and judicial structures. i think we have to be realistic when we speak about the possible outreach of this directive. this was something which was not only possible, but very ambitious. i want to thank the commission for their very forward-looking proposal for a directive, and thank the irish presidency for doing a very good job, including moving us towards compromise on very sensitive issues where member states had a number of concerns. finally, if this directive is implemented soon, it is going to have very visible results. i would therefore strongly encourage the member states not to wait the 18 months, but to go ahead with implementing the provisions of this directive in national legislation. then the european citizens will see that this is an instrument which is a game-changer for their lives, for democracy and for the digital economy.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to congratulate mr cashman on his excellent report and i should like to congratulate him on the fact that this second revision of the cotonou agreement retains the main objectives of our development policy. nevertheless, i would like to stress the need to maintain the coherence of a multidimensional approach, particularly in view of the next steps ahead for the 2015 and post-2020 review. the second revision reaffirms the role of economic partnership agreements in promoting economic development, but on the one hand we have seen that there has been little progress so far in concluding epas because negotiations are difficult, and on the other hand it seems that these agreements are not sufficient to meet all the challenges facing the acp countries. political dialogue remains a central element in our relations with this group and, as we know, economic growth is not enough for development. that is why i believe it is urgent, even though the deadline may seem distant, that the european union should define a clear position on what it wants for the future of the acp-eu partnership. in this context, i would like to ask two questions. in the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly, i proposed the creation of a working group on the future of cotonou. the acp-eu jpa, with its experience, could be very useful in defining the future of the cotonou agreement and i ask that its position be taken into account. secondly, each revision is based on a balance sheet and the last revision showed us that the national parliaments were rather left out of the process. can you assure us, commissioner, that the views of the national parliaments of the acp countries will be taken into account in the next steps?
mr president, i would like to support the motion of my colleague manfred weber. for 18 days, thousands of citizens in bulgaria have been protesting against the policies of the government of the bsp, dps and ataka. what we're seeing in bulgaria right now is this: on the one hand, the instability of the parliamentary majority, which can only function with the support of attack. second, an unprecedented suggestion by the speaker of the national assembly to restrict the freedom of the media in the country. third, lack of transparency in the overall decision-making process and appointments in the executive and legislative branches. a debate on these issues is necessary to show first of all the bulgarian citizens that their voice is heard, and here, as the european parliament, to send the message that we do not apply double standards to different countries and different ruling parties.
in bulgaria, people are on the streets. parliament has again failed to change the electoral code so that even the most ardent skeptics have faith in the election. international observers found serious violations in the most unfair and rigged elections, and the 350,000 ballots discovered were only part of the rigging that was being prepared. less than 50% voted, and 25% of them have no representation in parliament. fake elections create a fake parliament and a fake government, which lead to hunger, misery and despair. the biggest losers from the falsifications were from the national front for the salvation of bulgaria, who took 3.8% and barely got enough to enter parliament. they asked the court to annul the election, providing indisputable evidence of hundreds of violations in all the polling stations. therefore, the european parliament must intervene on the side of the suffering bulgarian parties, otherwise it will undermine democracy in bulgaria.
vice-president of the commission. - mr president, honourable members, i would like to thank the rapporteur mr marinescu for an excellent report and mr bach and mr sturdy for their contributions as rapporteurs for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs and the committee on international trade. aviation is a strategically important sector, as the rapporteur has already mentioned. the aviation industry supports over 5 million jobs and contributes more than eur[nbsp ]350 billion to european gdp. europe is a world leader in manufacturing and in aviation-linked research and development. but the european aviation sector is going through tough times. international competition, especially in air transport, is becoming fiercer and fiercer. the commission believes that the eu’s external aviation policy needs to change to meet these challenges. europe must maintain a strong and competitive aviation industry. over the past 10 years, we have shown that europe can make a difference. we have achieved results that never would have been possible without coordinated eu-wide action. but we can, and must, do much better. this is where the external aviation policy has an important role to play. so i am very pleased that this report strongly supports a more ambitious and better coordinated policy. it is time for the eu to act with one voice. this is a key issue. i would like to, if i may, single out one particular aspect: we need to be able to normalise our – difficult – aviation relations with russia, including on the famous question of siberian over-flights. i will therefore soon request a mandate from the council to open negotiations on a comprehensive eu-russia air transport agreement. your support for this would be helpful. russia seems to have been left out of paragraph[nbsp ]39 of the motion for a resolution which lists the countries with which the commission should negotiate a comprehensive agreement. i encourage you to include russia in that list.
i congratulate the rapporteur on the extensive and very substantive report that brings together the maritime sector policies. i would like to stress that blue growth could not be achieved unless a marine culture was first developed, which has a leading role in its implementation. it's a process that involves many institutions and organizations, education as well. however, there are no maritime subjects in the curricula, vocational schools related to maritime affairs are not particularly interesting, maritime professions are not considered attractive in higher education. to change this, maritime states need to have clearly defined priorities and strategies and to have a highly integrated maritime policy. its fragmentation across different institutions and an out-of-sync legislative framework have doomed it to failure. it is important to respect the model of the european union's integrated maritime policy. and i cannot help mentioning here, of course, that we have to go back to the issue of the black sea strategy and its future, which is extremely important.
in writing. ladies and gentlemen, i welcome the agreement reached on the 2014-2020 mff, which shows that the european institutions need to work together and not turn the eu budget into an interinstitutional hostage. the achievement of a compromise is key to ensuring a stable and predictable environment for investors as well as for member states when programming their budgets and preparing large long-term investment projects. in my view, the main shortcoming in the planning of the framework was not the volume of the resources committed, but the lack of a fundamental restructuring of its logic with a view to turning it into a long-term investment instrument - that is, to eliminate inefficient budget lines rather than simply adding new priorities in a systematic way. the challenges of today require that the mff is spent wisely, with a view to impact and 'added value' for our citizens and the eu as a whole. the european budget should not be the only means of achieving 'europe 2020', but it has the main role of driving and guaranteeing the achievement of the common objectives. in this context, i welcome the provision of greater flexibility to the budgetary framework, which will allow funds to be directed towards priority areas such as promoting youth employment, education and increasing the competitiveness of the european economy through investment in research and development. thank you, thank you!
mr president, mr prime minister, commissioners, the compromise reached on the multiannual financial framework was very difficult, not least because parliament had new functions, and i would like to ask the european council to get used to the new functions of parliament. the procedures that are required require co-decision on most issues and therefore the european parliament has not adopted and will not adopt presumed decisions that are taken by one side only, the council, without the european parliament being involved. as regards the content of the compromise reached, we cannot be satisfied with the budgetary levels that were envisaged. we are not satisfied because we continue to believe that they will not be able to finance the political objectives that the same prime ministers, heads of government and heads of state have set for the european union. at the same time, we are pleased that some of our main priorities, related to youth unemployment, related to competitiveness, related to the fund for the support of the poorest, were included in the last compromise that was made. i believe that important steps have also been taken in the area of budget reform. i would particularly like to stress the agreements for more flexibility and parliament will insist that every single euro that is included in the relevant budget ceilings be used for european policies. in addition, the mid-term budgetary review and own resources are also important issues on which parliament will press for concrete results. finally, i would like to stress that there are still a few conditions to complete the negotiations. the first condition is that the negotiations on the legal bases are completed and i would like to ask the council to open those topics which it has not yet opened in the field of agricultural policy and in the field of cohesion development, which were decided by the february council but on which parliament has the right to take joint decisions. and secondly, we will await the final decision on the budget update and the payment of all the payments that have to be made this year.

member of the commission. − madam president, the commission welcomes the solution found by parliament and the council at the conciliation committee meeting of 28 may. this compromise will not only enable a decision on the macro-financial assistance to georgia, which has been blocked for two years, but will also unblock union macro-financial assistance (mfa) for other strategic partners. in the commission’s view, macro-financial assistance will remain a crucial instrument in our relations with neighbouring and candidate countries. the achievement of its objective – economically to stabilise countries which are close to the eu – is more crucial than ever, given the negative impact of the euro debt crisis on our neighbourhood. the commission also welcomes the efforts by parliament and the council to set out guidelines and practical arrangements for future mfa operations in their joint declaration which is to be adopted together with the decision. the commission, acting in accordance with its right of initiative, will refer to these provisions both as overall guidelines and in relation to the specific situation of each beneficiary country. the commission hopes that, with an agreement on the mfa for georgia in place, the co-legislators can now swiftly respond to current mfa requests from the kyrgyz republic and jordan, as well as forthcoming ones, for instance from armenia and possibly tunisia.
on behalf of the alde group. madam president, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as a result of the worsening economic situation following the armed conflict between georgia and the russian federation, and the serious consequences of the global economic crisis, which is directly reflected in the georgian economy, the european union committed itself to providing macro-financial assistance to the country in 2008. as a liberal, i support the compromise reached between the institutions for georgia, on the principles laid down in the joint declaration on the establishment of common provisions for macro-financial assistance to third countries. the consultation procedure for aid up to the threshold of eur 90 million and the application of the commission review procedure for funds exceeding the threshold of eur 90 million, as well as the more stringent measures when the conditions for the management of the aid are not met, represent a good achievement from the point of view of interinstitutional balance. although, unfortunately, as our committee chairman mr. moreira mentioned, the framework regulation on macro-financial assistance to third countries was withdrawn by the commission at the last moment, even though a virtual agreement was reached between the two co-legislators on these issues. in the cases of ukraine and moldova, the european union's macro-financial assistance has proved to be an effective tool for economic stabilisation and has become a driver for structural reforms to pave the way for the development of an area of common security and prosperity. in the case of georgia, it is necessary for the country to take appropriate steps to prevent and combat fraud, corruption and other irregularities in relation to this assistance, and to take more decisive measures to respect human rights, the rule of law and the establishment of a multi-party system, which are essential preconditions for the granting of macro-financial assistance. in these circumstances, we urge the commission to improve the efficiency, transparency and accountability of macro-financial assistance. we must not forget that georgia is of strategic importance to the european union and we must help its economic and financial stabilisation.
madam president, commissioner, today it is extremely important to talk and do more about the european union's social policy, not just because of the crisis, but to provide a perspective for european citizens, to fight poverty. the report addresses many problems, many problem groups, and i would like to highlight two of the most important. first, low pay in education, health and social services. it is illogical to speak of intelligent growth, inclusive growth, when those on whom the educational and health status of the population depends are themselves of low standard. secondly, one group that needs special attention is that of women in rural areas. they are often isolated and marginalised, deprived of the opportunity to participate in the formulation of agricultural policies, isolated from social and health care systems, and denied real access to vocational training programmes and entrepreneurship. the depopulation of the villages and the encirclement of agriculture have reduced the rural woman to poverty and hopelessness.
mr president, for the first time in a long time, i am taking part in a debate which is under the consultation procedure. the reason why this part of the law is not in the co-decision procedure (the ordinary legislative procedure) is the concern of the member states that the european parliament, the european institutions can interfere with their sovereign rights, especially as was pointed out by many speakers - issues that are very ideologically charged and have very long traditions in the member states. but with these two reports before us, the european parliament is setting an example of not only being able to respect national traditions, but also to protect them to a large extent with the amendments that are being proposed. however, i would like to remind colleagues who speak on the subject of equality that these questions are beyond the scope of the reports under discussion, since we are talking about procedural law, the choice of the court of justice, the choice of legislation. so we cannot pass legislation on material matters. i would like to conclude by urging my colleagues to support these reports and, accordingly, to take our recommendations and amend the commission's proposal accordingly.
mr president, this amendment is an important step towards bringing the biofuels issue to a regulatory and very sensible measure. and this is not just about carbon emissions, but it's about a particularly pressing issue, particularly global food shortages. production of biofuels from energy feedstocks grown on food-producing land. in the debate last term, we insisted that the situation with agricultural land for biofuel production be thoroughly analysed. today it's clear that it wasn't done in the best way. it is important, however, to support the compromise today, because despite the differences that exist in the member states in terms of indirect land use, we must protect both biofuel production and the investments that have been made so far. biofuels, of course, are an important tool in the energy mix, and we need to maintain a balance between the three areas: environment, agriculture and economy.
in writing. − in writing. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, on several occasions i have already had the opportunity to express my position on mrs lepage's report, which is that the current level of scientific knowledge in this area cannot produce sufficiently solid results on which we could base legislation. economic models used to estimate the amount of indirect land-use change emissions give different results depending on the assumptions made, highlighting the need for a more precise methodology to measure these negative effects. it is particularly important to take into account, when amending the current directives, that the development of the biofuels sector has been driven to a large extent by eu policies in this area. we must be consistent in our actions, and that is why i am strongly against including a factor for npps in the calculation of greenhouse gas emissions. in addition to confirming the need to stimulate the wider uptake of next generation biofuels, we need to provide appropriate safeguards for investments already made in production capacity. the commission considers that the objective of the measure is to reduce the environmental impact of the use of biofuels by reducing the environmental impact of the use of biofuels.
in writing. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i congratulate mrs buzek on her comprehensive report, which realistically addresses the problems and proposes measures to achieve a truly functioning internal european energy market. two circumstances are particularly important for this energy market: the existence of sufficient technical connectivity between the parties and the operation of common rules for the functioning of the market. however, the necessary links for the bidirectional transmission of energy between the countries have not yet been established, nor have the documents of the third energy package been fully implemented. a serious problem is the lack of sufficient funding opportunities. therefore, i support the view that infrastructure projects for the construction of transmission capacity need public funding and i support the idea of allowing investment in such projects by pension funds and institutional investors in accordance with market principles. a study on the possibility of creating a special fund for investments in energy networks on the basis of a european consumption tax is also mandatory. i welcome the texts which require: - an urgent analysis of energy price formation in individual countries to clarify anti-competitive practices and the implications for consumer bills; - the abolition of the principle of indexing gas to oil prices and the renegotiation of old contracts based on outdated pricing mechanisms.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i just wish to underline that we do have european legislation and that, in the european semester, the commission has utilised this argument in the country-specific recommendations, so this work has been done. now let us come to the gender pay gap, which is a very complex problem with multiple causes. there are legal, social and economic factors which go far beyond the numbers in relation to equal pay for equal work. for instance, there is the under-valuation of women’s skills and competences, the difficulty for women of reaching higher positions, the unequal burden of family and domestic responsibilities, the fact of different workplace practices, and the issue of access to career development and training, as well as the actual structure of pay and job evaluation and job classification systems. there is also the fact of insufficiently strong action by the social partners and the very small number of cases being brought to court. my view – and we can discuss this when the commission puts its evaluation on the table – is that the gender pay gap is not an indicator of the overall inequality between men and women, because it concerns only earnings differences. it is interesting to see, for instance, that in countries where female employment is very low, the gender pay gap is much lower than average. why? because there is only a small proportion of low-skilled or unskilled women in the workforce. so we have to look at all this and we have to see how we can help the law to be better applied in the member states. that is what the commission will try to do, because we need to correct the ineffective implementation of the principle of equal pay at national level – a non-application, in fact, of the law – and to address the lack of a corrective system in society, with workers’ unions or courts having to come in unsolicited. this is something we need to discuss, and i am willing to do so together with parliament: to look at the facts and figures, because they are really interesting. if you go into the figures in depth, you will see that it is sometimes societies with the highest level of equality which have the widest gender pay gap. so there is something else which we should do, and i know that the austrian government, among others, has introduced transparency rules in the application of the directive in relation to the gender pay gap, in order to allow the social partners to participate and help to renegotiate structures within companies. i will put all this on the table and then we should decide how to proceed, because i absolutely agree with some of the speakers, who have been very clear here, that law alone does not solve the problem. it is a problem too multifaceted to be solved by two or three legal articles in a european directive. the facts have shown that the law may be written down, but it is not being applied. what are the reasons for this? that is the question we need to work on together in the coming months.
in writing. - women make up about 52% of the population and at least half of them are affected by the problem of unequal pay. unfortunately, the search for a solution is becoming more talk and less action. there are laws in the member states which do not imply such a distinction, but it does exist. this year, the pes women are running a targeted campaign to find a way out of the status quo, targeting the root causes of this inequality: the first. women hold lower-paid jobs - teachers, social workers, medical staff. these professions should be reassessed and whether they deserve such low financial status; 2. young women and women over 55 have limited access to employment in the labour market; 3. the majority of women continue to work without contracts and without full insurance, and even in these unfavourable conditions they are paid less than men. in the context of women's family care responsibilities, the problem of unequal pay should be treated as discrimination and sanctioned as such. attention should also be paid to the setting of standards for each type of work. once that's accomplished, we'll be able to limit the scale of the problem.
mr. president, according to the united nations, two million people have already fled syria. many more have left their homes and are potential emigrants. every day, about a hundred people try to enter the european union through the bulgarian-turkish border. most of them are syrians, mostly women and children. the capacity to accommodate immigrants in bulgaria is already fully filled. the expected deepening of the syrian conflict will inevitably lead to even greater immigration pressure. there are few options for action, as turkey has not yet ratified the readmission agreement with the european union and there is no way for the migrants to be returned. this situation can't go on much longer. the european union's position on the syrian crisis must include a solution for migrants. the union must increase its assistance to countries guarding the union's external border. the commitment to the arrival of migrants is the responsibility of the entire european union, not just the border countries. in the case of bulgaria, it is paradoxical that our country benefits from not being in schengen, because otherwise the pressure would increase significantly, which does not mean that we are not fulfilling all our commitments as a member of the european union. i want to appeal and ensure that the necessary funding for humanitarian aid is provided to keep the refugees close to the syrian border.
madam president, first of all i would like to thank and especially congratulate our colleague agustin díaz de mera garcía consuegra for his excellent work, but also for his commitment from the very beginning of this long process. this regulation has been the subject of debate for a long time, but it is thanks to its report that we have - i believe - succeeded in focusing on the very essence of our principles as a european union, an area of freedom and justice. the european parliament must also have a say in this. we now have a definition which helps to give us a solid basis for the future, because - we do not hesitate to say this - beyond the technical terms, we are sending a message of political importance as regards our relations with third countries. and to this must be added the neutral and objective criteria. it is thanks to these clarifications that any misuse could be prevented. on the technical side, any triggering of the suspension mechanism must be done in accordance with the criteria we have defined, but above all by a case-by-case analysis. this is the only way to respond adequately to an increase in migration, but above all to the danger of excessive exploitation. finally, the reciprocity mechanism should be strengthened to match our objective, namely the principle of efficiency. because unity of action and solidarity between the member states must remain the two main threads we must not lose sight of. the debate today proves this: we need a common visa policy with clearly defined instruments. that is how we can achieve our goals. i thank the rapporteur again for having enabled us to make important progress on this subject.

in writing. ladies and gentlemen, today, on the one hand, we have an imbalance between a progressively ageing population and low population growth in europe. on the other hand, 23.3% of young people in the eu are unemployed, about 29% of them have a real chance of finding a job, and there is a lack of staff for some 2 million vacancies in information technology, engineering, mathematics and science. this shows that youth unemployment is rooted in structural problems requiring adequate solutions and clear commitments from member states, the education system and business. member states should ensure a more flexible labour market, reduced administrative burden, potential tax breaks for companies employing young people, more opportunities for european, national and private funding, including risk capital for start-up companies and young entrepreneurs. educational institutions should reform educational programmes to include innovative teaching methods, a dual system, increased mobility and non-formal education, with the aim of building functionally literate young people with practical skills and an entrepreneurial spirit. businesses should be in direct contact with educational institutions, exercise corporate social responsibility policies and invest in human resources through training and apprenticeships. all of this can only be sustainable if young people are involved in the formulation of these decisions. thank you, thank you!
mr president, i would like to congratulate the commission and in particular vice-president kroes for having the vision and the courage to address this important issue of cybersecurity. this issue has been undervalued for a long time. it is not necessary for us to speak about it, about the digital agenda, about cloud computing, about 4g, 5g, without having a resilient infrastructure. we should be clear that the mere strategy will not guarantee the safety of the internet by itself. but the strategy will change the mindset, create a sensitivity and public knowledge and create an appropriate context for the legislation and the measures to stimulate further it development. i would like to see a more ambitious strategy, better coordination and even a new coordinator and more links to privacy, but we have to express our congratulations on the fact that the commission has moved from reaction to action and to impact on this important issue. security does not come with our regulation. security is about the ability to react and to be resilient. now, with the strategy, the commission should put pressure on the national governments and on the european institutions to enforce all the provisions that are in the strategy.
in writing. i voted in favour of this report because linguistic diversity is a treasure of europe's heritage which must be preserved. the report has the merit of proposing a number of concrete measures to protect endangered languages, including the potential of new media, the digitisation of documents, and giving priority to younger generations. linguistic diversity is a treasure for all european citizens, and it is also a commitment to defend these symbolic treasures because the european union is not just a large market. linguistic diversity must be used as a lever to assert europe's identity vis-à-vis the rest of the world, while ensuring that it is not divided within itself. it is a matter of respect for each other but also respect for our common european culture.
in writing - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the main objective of the proposal to amend the fuel quality and renewable energy directives is to avoid the negative effects of biofuel production linked to greenhouse gas emissions and indirect land use change. i regret to note that both the commission proposal and the report of the parliament's environment committee are based on questionable data on adverse effects and therefore propose inadequate measures. that's why i voted against the report. i consider it unacceptable at this stage of knowledge and experience to proceed with a substantial modification of the targets for first-generation biofuels and the introduction of mandatory reporting and calculation of emission factors and indirect land use effects, including penalties. this is far from an effective tool for addressing the negative effects. the proposal to introduce schemes and measures to prevent risks and adverse effects, including improper land use, to support the development of sustainable, risk-reduced practices for biofuel production was much more acceptable. i welcome the vote on a higher ceiling for first generation biofuels, as well as the setting of specific targets for second generation biofuels, which will preserve sustainable planning and investment in the development of new technologies.
in writing. i voted in favour of the report on the conclusion of the agreement between the eu and the republic of cape verde on the issue of short-stay visas. this agreement and the readmission agreement are the first agreements between an acp country and the eu on short-stay visas and the readmission of irregular residents. i have supported both reports because the agreements reached are of both political and procedural importance. the conclusion of these agreements is therefore an encouraging step forward and a strong signal from the eu. finally, i supported this report because an agreement to facilitate the issuing of visas is of great importance in the lives of citizens. visa liberalisation will reduce the time and costs of preparing for travel within the schengen area. the agreement also facilitates contacts between citizens, which is a key condition for the development of economic, cultural, scientific and other relations. the visa system also strengthens contacts between people and gives substance to the principle of free movement, which is one of the eu's fundamental rights.
in writing. i voted in favour of approving the conclusion of two agreements which have already been negotiated between cape verde and the european commission. the readmission agreement covers cape verde's readmission obligations applicable to national nationals as well as to third-country nationals and stateless persons. i have supported both of these agreements because they represent a very important step forward in the relations between the european union and the republic of cape verde. there is indeed a very clear desire for cape verde to be closer to the eu: in the early years of the 21st century, the cape verdean government and civil society have repeatedly expressed the desire to deepen and broaden their relations with the eu. finally, i voted in favour of both agreements, because the process of deepening relations between the eu and cape verde will certainly help to improve the administration of justice and the level of respect for freedoms. in addition, these two agreements are also of regional interest and will contribute to the eu's efforts to strengthen cooperation with other acp countries.
in writing. i voted in favour of this report because it makes the right findings and proposes concrete measures to improve european policy in favour of young professionals. it emphasises the need to rethink education in a more practical way, in line with the skills and knowledge sought in the labour market. there is a need for closer contacts between the labour market and the world of education. this is an important way forward and the exchange of good practice between member states should be encouraged. the report also stresses the need to encourage the professional mobility of young europeans: this is also an important solution, and we must find the means to implement it. this also means that restrictions on access to the labour market in other member states for all young europeans must be lifted. there should be no second-class citizens.
in writing − the large majority support for the report 'measures to tackle youth unemployment - possible solutions' shows the importance of the problem. the tone of concern was common to all the speeches, and the figures quoted were shocking: youth unemployment stands at 23% and in the countries most affected by the crisis, one in two young people is out of work. at the same time, there is a mismatch between the needs of the union labour market and the qualifications of jobseekers. actions to tackle high levels of youth unemployment cover the measures set out in the youth employment package (including youth opportunities and the youth employment initiative), the use of the structural funds, the promotion of eures, the development of a quality framework for vocational training. much remains to be done in the areas of prevention of early school leaving, improving labour mobility, promoting entrepreneurship (including through business start-up loans), encouraging businesses to hire young people. the system of traineeships must be structured in such a way as to ensure adequate remuneration for work. better coordination between the education sector on the one hand and the administration and business on the other is needed to overcome the existing mismatches.

vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. − mr president, honourable members, there is no question that the proposal which has been put forward to remove, destroy or put under observation the chemical weapons needs first of all to be verified, in the sense of ensuring that the assad regime is determined that it will be implemented and, as honourable members have said, that needs to be both in the meeting which will take place tomorrow and in our discussions: verified and ensured, in order that this is not a delaying tactic or anything other than an opportunity to help protect the people in syria. secondly, it is important that the un inspectors complete their work. their report, we believe, will be available in the coming days, and that will be an opportunity for everyone to see what happened and for greater verification to take place about the event. it will be an important moment when that report is put before the un security council. i have been talking with many countries, including iran, on the issues of chemical weapons and of how we can move forward in a diplomatic process to a conference that will bring together all those who are able to stop the fighting and move forward to a resolution that can be peaceful. the honourable members have just had the debate that i have seen so many times in so many places about military action: the questions about humanitarian corridors and no-fly zones, and how to enforce and achieve that. i have to tell you that, in all the conversations with the military experts that i have spoken to in many countries, we know that this is not something which is passive but active, or something that could be done easily. most importantly, we do not see that this is going to be on the agenda any time soon, as it has not been on the agenda over these past two years. but i do not want any misinterpretation that says that not to have military action is somehow to be passive. there is a huge amount of work going on all the time: meetings with so many different groups from syria, efforts being made to bring together the international community in a common position that can exert the kind of political pressure necessary to be able to bring people together in order to try and resolve this. the reason to do that, as so many honourable members have said, is for the sake of the millions of syrians who are suffering every single day – who see their lives shattered, their families destroyed, and their homes in ruins. that is why it is very difficult for anyone to imagine that a government that fails to be able to protect its people and indeed is complicit in murdering its people can continue. this does not suggest that there are not challenges for the future about building a peace process that will allow all communities to be part of the future of syria and to ensure that the values that originally brought the people out in demonstrations against the government two years ago must be the values upon which the new syria rises from the ashes. without that we will have failed the people of syria, the vast majority of whom want this war to end and want to find a peaceful way forward and to live in the kind of free societies that we enjoy. this is also important when you think about the context of refugees. honourable members have spoken about the need for member states to think about their responsibilities. we think all the time about the two million people – which is a huge number of people – who are now outside their country. frankly, the best way we can help them is to help them to be able to go home, and that means finding a way to end this war, to support the rebuilding of their country and to support the refugees. i am pleased and proud, that between us all, we are the biggest donor of humanitarian aid. i do not honestly think anybody here disagrees that this is fundamentally important – but we need to do more. this becomes very obvious when you, like me, have visited the refugee camps and seen people’s plight and the importance of getting schools to function for children, and the real importance of ensuring that people have the opportunity to do things and to feel that they will have a future where they can engage, in their own country, in the political process. it also means that we think about the misplaced people whose lives are miserable within the country and, again, who need to be helped and supported as much as possible. i have referred to the challenges of trying to get aid into the country: challenges that we have to face but which we have to overcome for their sake. any process is going to have to be led and developed through the united nations, but it is going to have to have within it the people who can bring this war to an end. that means it is about trying to put the right kind of programme together to build support from all the nations which have a stake in this and have a way in which they can ensure that peace comes and lasts. we do play our part and we will play our part. this does not mean that i am naive about what that role is, but it does mean that it is an important one. it is important because day after day we get messages from so many countries, organisations and people to ask us to help to do the things that we do well and that we will commit to do. finally, honourable members, i want to respond about camp ashraf, because vice-president vidal-quadras and mr salafranca raised that, and it is very important. this morning i left the chamber because i was talking to the iraqi foreign minister about two issues: firstly, the importance of ensuring that people are protected properly; the inquiry into the terrible massacre that we saw on 1 september, in which 52 people died, is under way, but i have urged that it be a properly-conducted inquiry. secondly, and just as importantly, the responsibility of the government to protect the people, and the seven people who are missing and who are being tracked down as we speak. we have offered our support through the new representative to the united nations, whom i have already met and indeed know well, but also of course to support the un process in any way that we can.
